sqlglot Composer: Example projects

An Example project is a Project made for the Example database's tables, laid out as yours is: made by the project module's Project actions, then filled in by hand. The Example database is nine made-up tables the Toolbox's makers keep, which the download doesn't hold, so the Example projects are shown here, not shipped as folders: read one to see what a Project holds once filled in. To start your own, run project.start(), as the README's step 1 says; the how-tos show each table the Example database holds.

For each: what it is, its folders, then each file. Each notebook was run top to bottom, as Jupyter's Run All runs it, in a fresh copy of its Project, and each code cell shows what it gave. A notebook connects at its top with connect(conn), your team's connection; here, it was the Example database's, which can be read but not written, so a write shows its Hive and changes nothing. A Workflow's notebook starts with its names cell empty, so it stops at the first step that needs a name: the how-tos run each one with names filled in, and the Workflows overview draws them. Each table's Lineage page is linked, to open in your browser.

Jump to the starter Example project and the intermediate Example project.

The starter Example project

The starter Example project

A Project made for the Example database's tables ops.jobs, ops.job_runs and ops.run_alerts, laid out as yours is. It is much like the Project the Getting started how-tos build, one step at a time, but not the same:

  • it also holds mart.alerts_per_job, which no how-to makes;
  • it lacks what the how-tos add to show one thing each: the Table references of ops.region_budgets and ops.team_notes, which Count, join and stay safe adds, mart.run_speed, which Follow lineage makes, and ops.scheduler_runs, which Keep the Project in order adopts;
  • its files are in full where a how-to shortens them, such as its Table references' comments.

It builds four Saved tables, each in a Table folder of its own, each made from the saved_table Template and partitioned by dt, the day, as ops.job_runs and ops.run_alerts are:

  • mart.alerts_per_severity: how many low and high alerts were raised each day. Its notebook is the Template filled in and nothing else: a count per group of the rows READS picks. Read it beside its Table reference, references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py, to see what each <...> became.
  • mart.daily_job_runs: each job's runs, failed runs and minutes per day, its rows from a Building block, building_blocks/runs_per_job.py, given READS.
  • mart.alerts_per_job: each job's alerts and high alerts per day, each alert joined to its run; its READS filters both tables.
  • mart.team_day: each team's day, read from mart.daily_job_runs and ops.jobs, so it is written after mart.daily_job_runs.

Each notebook's logic cell sets READS, the rows its write() reads, as the Template does:

Saved tableits write() reads
mart.alerts_per_severityequals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24"): the alerts raised on 2026-09-24
mart.daily_job_runsequals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"): the runs of 2026-09-24, through the Building block
mart.alerts_per_joball_of(equals(run_alerts.dt, "2026-09-24"), between(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-23", "2026-09-24")): the alerts raised on 2026-09-24, and the runs of that day and the day before, since a run can raise an alert after midnight
mart.team_dayequals(daily_job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"): the day mart.daily_job_runs saved

Each write fills the Partitions of the dt its rows hold, so to write another day, change the day in READS.

admin/check_project.ipynb

Check the Project

Reports, and changes nothing: a Table folder, Table reference, Building block or Scheduled run no Project action made (one made by hand), and one the registry lists that isn't there, and the next step of each Workflow you have started, in workflows/. project.do_next() runs the steps it can for you. Adopt one made by hand with project.register(...), given its table's name, or the Building block's or Scheduled run's.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parent))  # the Project: one up from admin/

from sqlglot_composer import project

It shows nothing.

Code cell 2

project.check()

It gives

Every Table folder, Table reference, Building block and Scheduled run is registered, and each one registered is there.

Code cell 3

project.do_next()  # runs the next steps it can of each Workflow you have started

It gives

No Workflow has a step left: each Workflow you started is done, or you have started none. To start one, name what it works on in the names cell of its notebook, in admin/workflows/, and save it.

Code cell 4

# project.register("mart.your_table")
# project.register("your_block")

It shows nothing.

admin/check_references.ipynb

Check the Table references

Compares every Table reference in references/ with its table in the warehouse, through your query, and prints what differs, with the line to change. Then it checks the Key of each table that has one: that no two rows share it. It changes nothing in the warehouse; its first cell refreshes the Reference sheet, so it writes references/reference_sheet.csv and the Database modules again and records the Project's Lineage state, and no other file. A Saved table you haven't created yet reports that DESCRIBE failed, as each of the Example projects' does: that is expected. It reads each table from the Reference sheet: its columns, their types, its partition columns and its Key. A partitioned table's Key is checked on its latest Partition, the last SHOW PARTITIONS lists, since checking every one would read the whole table; the last cell checks it on another you name. A Saved table's storage, its folder and file format, isn't in the sheet: the last cell checks it on the table's own Table reference, and the check prints the lines to write there.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parent))  # the Project: one up from admin/

from sqlglot_composer import project

It shows nothing.

Code cell 2

import csv

from sqlglot_composer import Table, all_of, check_key, check_table_reference, equals
from warehouse import connect, query

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

# The Reference sheet: a row per column of every Table reference in references/, with its
# type, and "yes" for a partition column and for a column in the Key.
print(project.refresh_reference_sheet())
sheet = pathlib.Path.cwd().parent / "references" / "reference_sheet.csv"
with open(sheet, encoding="utf-8", newline="") as file:
    rows = list(csv.DictReader(file))

It prints

Wrote references/reference_sheet.csv: 31 rows, 7 tables.

Code cell 3

# Each table, as the Reference sheet holds it, compared with the table in the warehouse;
# then its Key, where it has one.
for name in sorted({row["table"] for row in rows}):
    columns = [row for row in rows if row["table"] == name]
    partitions = [row["column"] for row in columns if row["partition"] == "yes"]
    key = [row["column"] for row in columns if row["in_key"] == "yes"]
    table = Table(name, columns={row["column"]: row["type"] or None for row in columns},
                  partitioned_by=partitions, key=key or None)
    found = check_table_reference(table, query=query)
    print(found)
    database, _, short = name.rpartition(".")
    if not key:
        print(f"{name} has no Key, so there is no Key to check.")
    elif not found.ok:
        print(f"{name}: its Key is checked once the problems above are fixed.")
    elif partitions:
        # Its Key on its latest Partition, of those SHOW PARTITIONS lists, such as
        # dt=2026-09-24: checking every one would read the whole table.
        shown = query(f"SHOW PARTITIONS {name}")
        partition_names = sorted(str(each) for each in shown.iloc[:, 0])
        if not partition_names:
            print(f"{name} holds no Partition yet, so there are no rows to check its Key on.")
        else:
            print(f"{name} is partitioned by {' and '.join(partitions)}, so its Key is checked "
                  f"on its latest Partition, the last of {len(partition_names)} that SHOW "
                  "PARTITIONS lists: checking every one would read the whole table.")
            on_the_latest = []
            for pair in partition_names[-1].split("/"):
                column, value = pair.split("=", 1)
                on_the_latest.append(equals(getattr(table, column), value))
            print(check_key(table, query=query, where=all_of(*on_the_latest)))
        if len(partition_names) > 1:
            column, value = partition_names[0].split("/")[0].split("=", 1)
            print("To check it on another, in the last cell:\n"
                  f"    from references.{database}__{short} import {short}\n"
                  f"    print(check_key({short}, query=query, "
                  f'where=equals({short}.{column}, "{value}")))')
    else:
        print(check_key(table, query=query))
    print()

admin/databases.py

Show its 13 lines
"""databases - the settings of the two Databases you own: DEV, where
you build and check tables, and PROD, where tables other people rely on are written. Set each
once; the Workflows read them.

"database" is the Database's name, such as "mart_dev". "location_root" is the folder its
tables' files go under: a table with no folder of its own goes in
<location_root>/<database>.db/<table>, such as /data/your_team/dev/mart_dev.db/daily_runs.
Any other setting is yours, such as "run_as", the account your connection runs as: give it
in both, each its own value, and database_settings("mart_dev") gives DEV's to your code.
"""

DEV = {"database": "mart", "location_root": "<DEV_LOCATION_ROOT>"}
PROD = {"database": "<PROD_DATABASE>", "location_root": "<PROD_LOCATION_ROOT>"}

admin/lineage.ipynb

The Lineage state and the Lineage difference

admin/lineage/lineage_state.csv, the Lineage state, records the logic of each column of your Project: its own logic and the rows that count for it, and its Effective logic, the same traced through every Saved table it reads, down to the tables the Project doesn't write. A row is added each time one changes, saying when, at which commit and from which call. Each Project action that changes a table or a Table reference records it already, and so does each Table notebook's cell 5, export_hive(): run the record cell below, project.record_lineage_state(), after a change made any other way, such as a Table reference edited by hand. project.check() says when the state is behind.

The difference cell after it writes the Lineage difference, admin/lineage/<time>-difference.html, and its Markdown twin: each column with an Own change, its own logic changed, and each with an Inherited change, its own logic the same but something it reads, up its chain, changed, with that chain down to the change it comes from, and when admin/sent.csv shows each changed table was next written. SINCE takes a time, such as "2026-11-20", or a commit, such as "1a2b3c4": left None, it compares the newest recording with the one before. TABLES takes a table's name, such as "mart.team_days", or a list of them, to show only those and the chains that reach them. Neither cell reaches the warehouse, so this notebook needs no connect(...).

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parent))  # the Project: one up from admin/

from sqlglot_composer import project

It shows nothing.

Code cell 2

project.record_lineage_state()  # after a change no Project action or cell 5 made

It gives

Lineage state: no change since the last recording.

Code cell 3

SINCE = None  # a time or a commit, such as "2026-11-20"; None: the one before
TABLES = None  # a table's name or a list, such as "mart.team_days"; None: all
project.export_lineage_difference(since=SINCE, tables=TABLES)

It gives

Wrote admin/lineage/20260925-060000-difference.html and its Markdown twin, 20260925-060000-difference.md: 1 table changed, since the recording of 2026-09-25 06:00:04, to the newest, of 2026-09-25 06:00:05: 5 columns (5 added).

admin/new_block.ipynb

New Building block

Writes a Building block, a piece several Table notebooks share, into building_blocks/<name>.py, from templates/building_block.py, and registers it. Its name is a Python name, such as runs_per_job, and a notebook's logic cell imports it by that name: from building_blocks.runs_per_job import runs_per_job. Refuses a name already taken. Never touches the warehouse. Name each in BLOCKS, run the cells, then fill in each new file's <...> and TODO lines. remove.ipynb archives one once no notebook imports it.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parent))  # the Project: one up from admin/

from sqlglot_composer import project

It shows nothing.

Code cell 2

BLOCKS = []  # each Building block to make, by its Python name, such as ["runs_per_job"]

It shows nothing.

Code cell 3

if BLOCKS:
    print(project.new_block(BLOCKS))

It shows nothing.

admin/refresh_reference_sheet.ipynb

Refresh the Reference sheet

Rebuilds references/reference_sheet.csv from every file in references/: one row per column, with its type, whether it is a partition column or in the Key, its Aliases and its description. A Table notebook's last cell refreshes only its own table's rows. It also writes each Database's file again, such as references/ops.py, so that from references import ops gives ops.jobs: run it once a Table reference is filled in.

admin/remove.ipynb

Remove

Moves Table folders, Table references, Building blocks, Conversion folders and Scheduled runs into admin/removed/, each named by the time, and logs it. Nothing is deleted, and a table stays in the warehouse: drop it with admin.drop_table in workflows/change_a_saved_table/. Name what to archive in the cell below, then run the rest: each list left empty is passed over.

admin/run_tables.ipynb

Run the tables

Writes every table: each Table notebook's write(), read from its logic cell with project.load, each before the tables that read it. First, with no execute, it prints each Hive in that order and sends nothing; then, with your query and execute, it sends them. tables= picks some; leave it out for every Table folder the registry lists. The cell under the Project's path connects to the warehouse, as every notebook that reaches it does: a Run value that reads the warehouse needs it even to print.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parent))  # the Project: one up from admin/

from sqlglot_composer import project

It shows nothing.

Code cell 2

from warehouse import connect, execute

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

It shows nothing.

Code cell 3

project.run_tables()  # first, with no execute: prints each Hive, sends nothing

It prints

-- 1 of 4: mart.alerts_per_severity
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.alerts_per_severity PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  run_alerts.severity,
  COUNT(*) AS alerts,
  run_alerts.dt
FROM ops.run_alerts AS run_alerts
WHERE
  run_alerts.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  run_alerts.severity,
  run_alerts.dt;
-- 2 of 4: mart.daily_job_runs
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
WITH runs_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS runs,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_runs.status = 'FAILED' THEN 1 END) AS failed_runs,
    SUM(job_runs.duration_mins) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  WHERE
    job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_runs.job_id,
    job_runs.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.daily_job_runs PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  runs_per_job.job_id,
  runs_per_job.runs,
  runs_per_job.failed_runs,
  runs_per_job.minutes,
  runs_per_job.dt
FROM runs_per_job;
-- 3 of 4: mart.alerts_per_job
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.alerts_per_job PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  job_runs.job_id,
  COUNT(*) AS alerts,
  COUNT(CASE WHEN run_alerts.severity = 'high' THEN 1 END) AS high_alerts,
  run_alerts.dt
FROM ops.run_alerts AS run_alerts
JOIN ops.job_runs AS job_runs
  ON job_runs.run_id = run_alerts.run_id
WHERE
  run_alerts.dt = '2026-09-24'
  AND job_runs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-23' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_runs.job_id,
  run_alerts.dt;
-- 4 of 4: mart.team_day
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.team_day PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  jobs.team,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.failed_runs) AS failed_runs,
  SUM(daily_job_runs.minutes) AS minutes,
  daily_job_runs.dt
FROM mart.daily_job_runs AS daily_job_runs
JOIN ops.jobs AS jobs
  ON jobs.job_id = daily_job_runs.job_id
WHERE
  daily_job_runs.dt = '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  jobs.team,
  daily_job_runs.dt;

It gives

Printed 4 writes in order, and sent nothing. Pass execute= to run them.

admin/scheduled_runs.ipynb

Scheduled runs, by hand

What no Scheduled run does each time it runs, since it would be work for nothing each time: run these cells by hand, when you want them. To make a Scheduled run, use the schedule Workflow, workflows/schedule/schedule.ipynb. project.check() reports a loop among your tables, two or more each waiting on another so that none can be written first, naming the logic cell to mend, and changes nothing. project.export_project_lineage() writes lineage/<time>-project.html and its Markdown twin: every Saved table, what reads it, and which Scheduled runs write it, a table more than one writes marked and a loop drawn red. It builds each Scheduled run as its Build cell does, with its parameters' defaults, and sends nothing. Name Scheduled runs in RUNS for each one's own: project.export_run_lineage(RUNS) writes its lineage into its folder's lineage/, and project.export_run_hive(RUNS) its built Hive, in order, into <name>.hql beside its notebook, to read, never to edit. Name tables in TABLES for project.describe_logic(TABLES): each logic function of their Table notebooks, its hints and its docstring's first sentence, to paste under Copilot file 18. Each list left empty is passed over. The cell under the Project's path connects to the warehouse: a Run value that reads the warehouse needs it even to build.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parent))  # the Project: one up from admin/

from sqlglot_composer import project

It shows nothing.

Code cell 2

from warehouse import connect

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

It shows nothing.

admin/templates/building_block.py

Show its 56 lines
"""<BLOCK> - TODO: say in one line what one row of it is.

Fill in every <...> in the code below this text, brackets and all: Python stops at one left
in. They are:
    <SOURCE_NAME> and <SOURCE_TABLE>: the table it reads, by its file in references/ and its
        name there, such as ops__job_runs and job_runs.
    <GROUP_COLUMN>: the column each row is for, such as job_id.
    <NUMBER_COLUMN>: a column of numbers to add up, such as duration_mins.
    <TOTAL_NAME>: the name of what it adds up to, in quotes, such as "minutes".

Why: TODO: which tables' notebooks read it, so they can never work it out two ways.

project.new_block("<BLOCK>") wrote this file from admin/templates/building_block.py. A
Building block is a piece several notebooks share; this one is a Derived table, a Statement
given a name, which each Statement reading it writes at its top, as WITH <BLOCK> AS (...). In a
notebook's logic cell, import it, and read it like a table, given the rows it reads, such
as READS, the condition at the top of a saved_table notebook's logic cell:

    from building_blocks.<BLOCK> import <BLOCK>


    def preview() -> Statement:
        per_group = <BLOCK>(READS)
        return statement(SELECT(per_group.<GROUP_COLUMN>, per_group.row_count),
                         FROM(per_group))
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>  # the Table reference it reads


def <BLOCK>(reads: Condition) -> Table:  # named as its file is
    """One row per group of the rows `reads` picks: its rows, and its total.

    `reads` is a condition on the table it reads, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24").
    On a partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns.
    """
    return derived(
        "<BLOCK>",  # the name the Hive gives it, the same as its file's
        statement(
            SELECT(
                <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
                # to keep each Partition's rows apart, add its partition column here and in
                # GROUP_BY, such as job_runs.dt
                AS(count_rows(), "row_count"),
                # add a line like the next for each number to add up
                AS(sum_of(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<NUMBER_COLUMN>), <TOTAL_NAME>),
            ),
            FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(<SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>),
        ),
    )

# %% [markdown]
# # <DB>.<TABLE>
#
# Fill in every `<...>` in the code cells below, brackets and all: the notebook won't run with
# one left. They are:
#   - `<CHECKED_NAME>` and `<CHECKED>`: the table checked, by its file in references/ and
#     its name there, such as `ops__job_events` and `job_events`;
#   - `<READS>`: which of its rows are checked, as a condition on it, such as
#     `equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24")` or `equals(region_costs.region, "eu")`. On a
#     partitioned table, it must filter one of its partition columns. A table that isn't
#     partitioned can be read whole: delete the line `READS = <READS>` and each line
#     `WHERE(READS),` instead;
#   - `<KEY_COLUMN>`: the column that picks out one row, such as `event_id`;
#   - `<PARTITION_COLUMN>`: the column of the table checked that fills this table's partition
#     column, such as `dt` or `region`. If this table isn't partitioned, delete the two lines
#     of rows() naming `<PARTITION_COLUMN>`, and write `PARTITIONED_BY = []`, instead.
#
# ## Terms
#
# - **Logic**: the code cell that starts `# logic`, cell 2. It only imports and defines, so
#   `project.load("<DB>.<TABLE>")` can run it from anywhere else, for lineage across your
#   tables and for `admin/run_tables.ipynb`, which sends its `write()`.
# - **Table reference**: `references/<NAME>.py`, the script describing this table: its
#   columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
# - **Partition**: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned
#   by.
# - **Key**: the columns whose values name exactly one row of the table.
# - **Decision**: one thing about this table that only you decide, such as its location; its
#   Table reference says whether it is made.
#
# ## Steps
#
# 1. **Fill in each `<...>`.** Goal: logic that runs. Action: type over each one listed above,
#    in the code cells. Done when no code cell holds a `<...>`; then step 2.
# 2. **Save the notebook.** Goal: the logic its Table reference is written from. Action: save
#    it. Done when it is saved; then step 3.
# 3. **Write its Table reference.** Goal: a Table reference written from the logic. Action:
#    run `project.reference_from_logic("<DB>.<TABLE>")`, a new_table Workflow step, which
#    writes `references/<NAME>.py` from the logic, for you to answer.
#    It lists one column, "row_count", a count, "bigint", then the partition column counted,
#    typed as the table checked types it, as PARTITIONED_BY names it. Its Key, proposed from
#    the logic, is that partition column: one row per Partition counted.
#    Done when `references/<NAME>.py` lists those columns; then step 4.
# 4. **Answer its Decisions.** Goal: each Decision made. Action: answer each open Decision its
#    Table reference leaves, such as its location, in the new_table Workflow's decisions
#    cell. Done when its Table reference leaves no Decision open; then step 5.
# 5. **Say what one row is.** Goal: a reader knows what the table holds. Action: write one
#    line saying what one row of <DB>.<TABLE> is, in place of the TODO line below. Done when
#    no TODO line is left in this cell; then step 6.
# 6. **Run the cells.** Goal: the logic tried, its lineage and Hive written, and its rows in
#    the Reference sheet. Action: run the code cells top to bottom, once its Table reference
#    is written from the logic. Done when each runs with no error, and cell 3 shows the rows
#    `preview()` gives.
#
# ## This table
#
# TODO: say in one line what one row of <DB>.<TABLE> is.
#
# Quality checks on a table you read. The row count of each of its Partitions READS picks is
# saved, so you can read them side by side and see one that looks too small; and checks run on
# the same rows: repeated_keys looks for a key with more than one row, and null_counts counts
# NULLs.
#
# Made by `project.new_table("<DB>.<TABLE>", template="quality_checks")` from
# `admin/templates/quality_checks.py`, which filled in the database, the table and its file's name,
# <NAME>.
#
# ## The code cells
#
# - 1: the Project, your connection, connected with `connect(...)`, and your query.
# - 2: the logic, which starts `# logic`.
# - 3: try it.
# - 4: its lineage.
# - 5: its Hive, in <NAME>.hql.
# - 6: its rows in the Reference sheet.
#
# ## Self-check
#
# - No code cell holds a `<...>`.
# - `references/<NAME>.py` lists the columns `write()` saves, and leaves no Decision open.
# - One line above says what one row is, and no TODO line is left.
# - Each code cell runs top to bottom with no error.

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import connect, query

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
# connect(your_connection)

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it checks, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, HAVING, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, WHERE, Clause, Column,
    Statement, all_columns, count_rows, create_table, equals, is_null, more_than, statement,
)
from references.<NAME> import <TABLE>  # this table's own Table reference, of row counts
# the table checked, such as: from references.ops__job_events import job_events
from references.<CHECKED_NAME> import <CHECKED>

# The rows checked: change it to check others. A condition that calls something other
# than equals, such as between or is_in, needs it added to the import above.
READS = <READS>

# This table's partition columns, in the order its SELECT gives them, last: [] for none.
PARTITIONED_BY = ["<PARTITION_COLUMN>"]


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create <DB>.<TABLE> from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(<TABLE>, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> list[Clause]:
    """The row count of each Partition READS picks: the clauses preview and write share. The
    partition column comes last in SELECT, so INSERT_OVERWRITE writes each count into the
    Partition of the same value, replacing only those."""
    return [
        SELECT(
            AS(count_rows(), "row_count"),
            <CHECKED>.<PARTITION_COLUMN>,  # the Partition counted: this table's partition column
        ),
        FROM(<CHECKED>),
        WHERE(READS),
        GROUP_BY(<CHECKED>.<PARTITION_COLUMN>),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows())


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3: save the row counts, replacing the Partitions they fill."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(<TABLE>), *rows())


def repeated_keys() -> Statement:
    """Each key that picks out more than one of the rows READS picks: it should find no
    rows."""
    return statement(
        SELECT(<CHECKED>.<KEY_COLUMN>, AS(count_rows(), "times_seen")),
        FROM(<CHECKED>),
        WHERE(READS),
        GROUP_BY(<CHECKED>.<KEY_COLUMN>),
        HAVING(more_than(count_rows(), 1)),  # tested once the rows of each key are counted
    )


def column_name(column: Column) -> str:
    """A column's own name: a column prints as its table and its name, such as
    job_events.minutes, and this keeps the part after the dot."""
    return str(column).split(".")[-1]


def null_counts() -> Statement:
    """How many of the rows READS picks hold NULL in each column, as one row: null_minutes
    counts the rows whose minutes is NULL, and so on."""
    counts = [AS(count_rows(where=is_null(column)), f"null_{column_name(column)}")
              for column in all_columns(<CHECKED>)]
    return statement(
        SELECT(*counts),
        FROM(<CHECKED>),
        WHERE(READS),
    )

# %%
# 3. Try it: the Hive of each step, then the rows
show_hive([create(), write()])
run_query(preview(), query)
run_query(repeated_keys(), query)  # should have no rows
run_query(null_counts(), query)

# %%
# 4. Lineage: a new pair in lineage/, named by the time and this table, beside the ones before
export_lineage(write())

# %%
# 5. Its Hive: <NAME>.hql beside this notebook, from the logic as last saved, then the
#    Project's Lineage state recorded: run it after each change to the logic
export_hive()

# %%
# 6. The Reference sheet: this table's rows, from references/<NAME>.py
project.refresh_reference_sheet("<DB>.<TABLE>")

# %%
# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import connect, query

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
# connect(your_connection)

# %%
# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the rows it reads, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, WHERE, Clause, Statement, count_rows,
    create_table, equals, statement,
)
from references.<NAME> import <TABLE>  # this table's own Table reference
# each table it reads, such as: from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs
from references.<SOURCE_NAME> import <SOURCE_TABLE>

# The rows write() reads: change it to write others. A condition that calls something other
# than equals, such as between or is_in, needs it added to the import above.
READS = <READS>

# This table's partition columns, in the order its SELECT gives them, last: [] for none.
PARTITIONED_BY = ["<PARTITION_COLUMN>"]


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create <DB>.<TABLE> from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(<TABLE>, may_exist=True)


def rows() -> list[Clause]:
    """The clauses preview and write share, so the two can never disagree: a count per group
    of the rows READS picks.

    A partitioned table's partition columns come last in SELECT, in its Table reference's
    order, named as it names them: INSERT_OVERWRITE fills each Partition from the rows' own
    values, and replaces only the Partitions they fill. They stay in GROUP_BY too, so each
    Partition's rows stay apart.
    """
    return [
        SELECT(
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,  # what each row is for
            AS(count_rows(), <COUNT_NAME>),
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<PARTITION_COLUMN>,  # this table's partition column, last
        ),
        FROM(<SOURCE_TABLE>),
        WHERE(READS),
        GROUP_BY(
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<GROUP_COLUMN>,
            <SOURCE_TABLE>.<PARTITION_COLUMN>,  # kept, so each Partition's rows stay apart
        ),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows())


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3: save the rows, replacing the Partitions they fill, or the whole table if it
    isn't partitioned: sent twice, it leaves the same rows, not twice as many."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(<TABLE>), *rows())

# %%
# 3. Try it: the Hive of each step, then the rows
show_hive([create(), write()])
run_query(preview(), query)

# %%
# 4. Lineage: a new pair in lineage/, named by the time and this table, beside the ones before
export_lineage(write())

export_hive()

# 6. The Reference sheet: this table's rows, from references/<NAME>.py
project.refresh_reference_sheet("<DB>.<TABLE>")

    <COLUMNS>: the columns write() saves and their types, from its logic's preview().

Why: TODO: who reads this table, and why it is saved rather than worked out each time.

until the table's logic is written: then project.reference_from_logic writes it again, its
columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its
query makes. A Saved table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it from this
file, so nothing can be asked of it: its logic says what it holds.
"""

    "<DB>.<TABLE>",
    columns={"<COLUMNS>": "string"},  # project.reference_from_logic writes them, from the logic
    location=None,  # TODO: its own folder, such as "/data/your_team/<DB>.db/<TABLE>"
)

admin/templates/scheduled_run.py

Show its 117 lines
# %% [markdown]
# # Scheduled run: <NAME>
#
# Fill in every `<...>` in the code cells below, brackets and all: the notebook won't run with
# one left. They are:
#   - `<RESULT_NAME>`: the name a SELECT's rows come back by, in quotes, such as
#     `"week_totals"`;
#   - `<SAVED_TABLE>`: a Saved table whose Table notebook's logic it uses, such as
#     `mart.team_days`;
#   - `<LOGIC_FUNCTION>`: the function of that logic it calls, with what it takes, such as
#     `week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY)`, two parameters, or `write()`.
#
# Follow the steps in order, as written.
#
# ## Terms
#
# - **Scheduled run**: this notebook, which an outside scheduler runs top to bottom: building
#   the Statements and their Hive in the Build cell, then sending them in the Run cell.
# - **Parameter**: a value the scheduler passes in, one of the names in the `# parameters`
#   cell, read by its type hint: `"2026-11-01"` given for a `datetime.date` is that date.
# - **Run value**: what a Python function gives each time a Statement is built, such as the
#   latest Partition of a table; `run_values=` gives one a value of yours for this run.
#
# ## Steps
#
# 1. **Name the parameters.** Goal: each value the scheduler passes in. Action: write each in
#    the `# parameters` cell as a name, its type hint and its default, such as
#    `RUN_DATE: datetime.date | None = None`; keep `START_FROM`. Done when each has a hint;
#    then step 2.
# 2. **Connect.** Goal: this run's account. Action: in the setup cell, write your connection
#    in the `connect(...)` line and delete its `#`. Done when the setup cell runs; then step 3.
# 3. **Name the Statements.** Goal: what this run sends. Action: write each in the dict
#    `statements()` gives, in the `# scheduled` cell, by name. If it is a Table notebook's
#    logic, load it: `project.load("mart.team_days").week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY)`. If
#    it is your own, compose it there, with `statement(...)`. Done when each is named; then
#    step 4.
# 4. **Build.** Goal: the Hive, in order, to read before anything is sent. Action: run the
#    cells down to the Build cell. If a table it would write is written elsewhere, name it in
#    `leave_out=`. Done when it prints each Statement's Hive in order; then step 5.
# 5. **Run.** Goal: the Statements sent and the SELECTs' rows back. Action: run the Run cell,
#    or have the scheduler run the notebook. Done when it ends with no error; each SELECT's
#    rows are in `results` by the name `statements()` gives it, such as
#    `results["week_totals"]`.
#
# ## What the build prints
#
# First each parameter as used, then each Statement in the order it is sent, headed like
# `-- 3 of 7: mart.team_days (read by week_totals)`, and its Hive. The writes of each table of
# this Project the Statements read are added, all the way up, before what reads them.
#
# Made by `project.new_scheduled_run("<NAME>")` from `admin/templates/scheduled_run.py`.
#
# ## Self-check
#
# - No code cell holds a `<...>`.
# - Each parameter in the `# parameters` cell has a type hint.
# - The Build cell prints each Statement's Hive, in order, and sends nothing.

# %%
# parameters
# The values a scheduler passes in, each a name, its type hint and its default. A scheduler
# passes text, which the Build cell reads by the hint, and refuses a value it can't read. The
# hints it reads: datetime.date, int, float, bool, str, each alone, in list[...] or with
# | None, such as list[datetime.date] for "2026-11-01,2026-11-08".
import datetime  # for a datetime.date hint

START_FROM: str = ""  # a table or SELECT to start from, as a failed run prints it; "" sends all
# RUN_DATE: datetime.date | None = None  # such as "2026-11-01", passed in as text

# %%
# Setup: the Project on Python's path, the Toolbox and your connection
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import Statement, project, statement
from warehouse import connect, execute, query

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
# connect(your_connection)

# %%
# scheduled
# The Statements this run sends, each by its name: a SELECT's rows come back in results by it.
# A Table notebook's logic gives them through project.load, such as
#     "week_totals": project.load("mart.team_days").week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY),
# and a Statement of your own is composed here with statement(...). A value worked out each
# time the run is built goes in as run_value(function), a function of this cell, such as
# week_totals(run_value(week_start), ...): import run_value from sqlglot_composer here.


def statements() -> dict[str, Statement]:
    """What this run sends, by name: the build adds the writes of each table they read."""
    return {
        "<RESULT_NAME>": project.load("<SAVED_TABLE>").<LOGIC_FUNCTION>,
    }

# %%
# Build: the Statements, in order, and their Hive, printed; nothing sent
# It reads the parameters first, then builds each Statement, adding the writes of each table
# of this Project they read. run_values={"period_end": RUN_DATE} gives a parameter of yours,
# RUN_DATE, to each Run value whose function is named period_end, in the writes added too;
# leave_out=["mart.daily_job_runs"] leaves out that table's write, and what only it reads.
built = project.build_run(statements, run_values={}, leave_out=[])

# %%
# Run: each one sent, in the build's order
# Each write is sent through execute and each SELECT through query; each SELECT's rows are in
# results by the name statements() gives it, such as results["week_totals"]. One that fails
# stops the rest, and the run prints the START_FROM to send the rest from.
results = built.run(query, execute)

admin/workflows/change_a_saved_table/change_a_saved_table.ipynb

change_a_saved_table

Changes a Saved table in the warehouse with one of the admin module's Admin statements, each logged in admin/admin_log.md.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Admin statement: a Statement from the Toolbox's admin module that changes a Saved table's definition or deletes its data, such as dropping some of its Partitions; it shows what it will change before its Hive.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.
  • Statement: a query your code builds, which becomes one Hive string.

Before step 1

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so do project.check(), which names the next step, and project.do_next(), the cell before the last.

Decision rules

  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell in turn, in order.
  • If you want them run for you, run the last cell, project.run_workflow(...), in place of their cells. It runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped: go on from that step.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the cell before the last. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Step 1's cell sets change with its arguments, no ... left.
  • admin/sent.csv records the Admin statement on TABLE, and admin/admin_log.md logs it.
  • You ran the check its Check first line names, and any check after it.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

admin/workflows/convert/convert.ipynb

convert

Turns Hive someone else wrote, one query or a batch of scripts, into your own code: each table the scripts write becomes a Saved table in your Dev Database, with its Table folder, and what only you can decide of it, a Decision, answered in the decisions cell.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.
  • Batch: the scripts converted together, under one name.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.
  • Table folder: the folder in tables/ that holds everything about one Saved table: its Table notebook, its past lineage, and its Hive.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Original: the table a converted script writes, in the warehouse already, which its Saved table copies; for a query alone, such as one Copilot wrote from a workbook, the rows the query gives.
  • Table notebook: the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create, preview and write its Saved table; its logic cell is the one whose first line is # logic.

Before you start

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so does project.check(), which names the next step.

Decision rules

  • If you want the steps run for you, as most do: fill in the names cell and save the notebook, run "Run up to your Decisions", paste what it prints into the decisions cell under it, fill it in and save, then run "Run the rest". Each runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped.
  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell under "Step by step", in turn, in order.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the last cell. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Each table the scripts write has its Table folder in tables/ and its Table reference in references/.
  • Each table's conversion/checklist.md says None under Open Decisions.
  • admin/sent.csv records each table created and written, and each History table seeded first.
  • Each table's conversion/compare.md says it passed, and the batch's folder is in admin/workflows/convert/finished/.
  • project.check() names no next step of this batch, and names it under Done.

Code cell 2

NAME = ''  # the batch's name, plain lowercase, such as "weekly" (or, for the run cells alone, a list of batches converted already); unused when EACH_SCRIPT_ITS_OWN_BATCH is True, each batch named for its file
FILES = []  # the scripts' files, such as ["daily.hql"], or an Excel workbook's, such as ["runs.xlsx"], each calculation of it a Saved table, each beside this notebook or a path anywhere, such as "C:/work/daily.hql", read where it is: left [], every .hql file beside this notebook, never a workbook, which you name here; or HIVE when it is given
HIVE = None  # the Hive as text, such as """SELECT ...""", or None to read FILES
EACH_SCRIPT_ITS_OWN_BATCH = False  # True makes each file of FILES a batch of its own, named for its file without .hql, such as "daily" for daily.hql, for simple scripts that read none of each other's tables: the run cells run each in turn, and one decisions cell answers them all
DATABASE = 'default'  # the database of a table the Hive names without one
RENAME = {}  # a name of your own for a table's copy in dev, by the table's full name, such as {"mart.team_fails": "team_fails"} for mart_dev.team_fails: left out, a copy is <table>_<its database>, such as mart_dev.team_fails_mart
STAGING = {}  # each staging table convert names, kept or folded, such as {"ops_reports.failed": "keep"}: left out, a temporary or DROPped one is folded, any other kept
PARTITIONS = []  # the Partitions for step 6 to compare besides the latest, each a dict, such as [{"dt": "2026-09-23"}], each value with no quotes of its own: [] compares only the latest Partition your dev table and its original both hold

DECISIONS = {}  # your answer to each open Decision, by the table's name as
# project.check() writes it, such as
# {"mart_dev.weekly_ops": {"key": ["job_id"], "types": {"fails": "bigint"},
#                          "tags": {"PERIOD_END": "'2026-10-31'"}}}
# "key": None says no columns name one row. project.check() names each one open, and
# prints a whole DECISIONS to paste over this line: fill in each <FILL IN>. A Key
# convert or new_table proposed, marked # proposed, is kept unless you answer "key"
# here; one add_references proposed stays open until you accept it.
# "key": "proposed" accepts either as if typed. "all_tables" answers for every table
# of this notebook, and a table's own answer overwrites it, kind by kind and tag by
# tag:
# {"all_tables": {"key": "proposed",
#                 "tags": {"PERIOD_END": "'2026-10-31'"}}}
# accepts each table's proposed Key and gives each table holding PERIOD_END that
# value. A table's location is placed under "location_root" in
# admin/databases.py; answer "location" only for one it can't place.
# "kind" says whether each write replaces the whole table, "rebuilt", or keeps the
# rows already there, "history", as a daily table keeps each day: "proposed" accepts
# the kind proposed from its write, which project.check() names, and under
# "all_tables" accepts each table's.
# A tag may be answered with a Run value's function of your Project's, imported above
# DECISIONS, such as "PERIOD_END": period_end. For a table's latest Partition, write
# project.new_run_value("period_end", latest_partition="ops.job_runs.dt") at the top
# of this cell, the import it prints under it, and run the cell once: it writes the
# function into building_blocks/run_values.py. And "seed", such as
# {"dt": {"from": "2026-01-01"}}, limits the Partitions a table that reads itself
# copies: how-to 09 shows both.

Step by step

Each step's own cell, to run or run again one step on purpose; the cells above run them all for you.

1. Convert the scripts: each table they write a Saved table in dev

It is a call to the warehouse, yours to run.

  • Goal: a Table folder and a Table reference for each table the scripts write, and for each calculation of a workbook that nothing else in it reads, each a Saved table in your Dev Database.
  • Action: Run the cell below. It reads DESCRIBE through your query, and writes only files.
  • Done when admin/workflows/convert/ holds the batch's folder, under its name, with its batch.md, which convert writes last; then step 2.

Code cell 6

project.convert(NAME, HIVE, files=FILES, query=query, database=DATABASE,
                rename=RENAME, staging=STAGING)

It stops with this message

ValueError:
  What happened:  project.convert("", ...): '' isn't a plain lowercase name.
  Why it matters: A batch's folder is named by it, and a query alone's Saved table, in your Dev Database, apart from a table's name, which holds a dot.
  Usual fix:      Use lowercase letters, digits and _, starting with a letter, such as "team_runs".
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

2. Answer each table's open Decisions in the decisions cell

It is a decision, yours to make.

  • Goal: an answer to each open Decision of the batch's tables, each Script tag's value among them.
  • Action: Run the cell below, project.check(): it names each open Decision and prints a whole DECISIONS to paste over the decisions cell's; then write each answer in the decisions cell, DECISIONS, by the table's name as project.check() writes it, each <FILL IN> given its answer, then save the notebook. Each table's conversion/checklist.md, in its Table folder, lists its open Decisions. A Script tag, a <PARM="NAME"/> the program that ran the scripts, such as a scheduler, replaced with a value before Hive read it, is answered under "tags" with that value as Hive. A tag standing alone, as in dt <= <PARM="PERIOD_END"/>, is answered with the value's own quotes: {"tags": {"PERIOD_END": "'2026-10-31'"}}. A tag already inside quotes, as in '<PARM="SKIP_TEAM"/>', is answered without them: {"tags": {"SKIP_TEAM": "nobody"}}. To change one, answer it again and run steps 3, 5 and 6 again: the table is made already, so 4 stays done.
  • Done when the decisions cell answers each Decision project.check() names for this batch; then step 3.

Code cell 7

project.check()

Not run: a cell above stopped, as Run All stops.

Its report lists each day not loaded, with the warehouse's error, and each that wrote no rows. A Rebuilt table has no Seed, and running the cell again loads nothing twice. Then project.run_tables sends each table's write() through your execute.

6. Compare each table with its original

It is a call to the warehouse, yours to run.

  • Goal: each table's rows checked against its original's: a partitioned table's on the latest Partition both hold, and on each other one PARTITIONS lists.
  • Action: Run the cell below. project.compare_batch reads both through your query and writes each table's result in its conversion/compare.md. A partitioned table is compared on the latest Partition both tables hold, which its line in the report names, such as on dt = '2026-09-24'. If you want other Partitions compared too, each one your dev table holds as well as its original, then list them in PARTITIONS in the names cell, such as [{"dt": "2026-09-23"}], each value with no quotes of its own, run the names cell, and run this step's cell again: each table partitioned by dt is compared on its latest and on each listed, the others on their latest, each result its own section, kept beside the ones before; a result that failed is replaced when that Partition is compared again. If the batch is finished already, then the same: list them in PARTITIONS and run this step's cell, or "Run the rest", which compares a finished batch again whenever PARTITIONS lists a Partition, and says the batch was finished.
  • Done when every result in each table's conversion/compare.md says Passed, and the batch's folder has moved to admin/workflows/convert/finished/; a table with a result that failed is one promote refuses; the Workflow is done: go through the Self-check at the top.

admin/workflows/new_table/new_table.ipynb

new_table

Makes a Table folder for each new Saved table: from its query, when QUERIES gives one, its logic written for you; else from a Template, its logic for you to write. A Building block, a piece several notebooks share, is made with project.new_block(...).

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Table folder: the folder in tables/ that holds everything about one Saved table: its Table notebook, its past lineage, and its Hive.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Template: the empty shape of a Table notebook, in admin/templates/, each specific left as a <...> to fill in.
  • Building block: a piece of your own code several notebooks share, a file of its own in building_blocks/.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Batch: the scripts converted together, under one name.
  • Table notebook: the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create, preview and write its Saved table; its logic cell is the one whose first line is # logic.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Original: the table a converted script writes, in the warehouse already, which its Saved table copies; for a query alone, such as one Copilot wrote from a workbook, the rows the query gives.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.

Before you start

Each runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped.

Step by step

Each step's own cell, to run or run again one step on purpose; the cells above run them all for you.

1. Make each Table folder, converting each table's query QUERIES gives

It changes only files, so project.do_next() runs it for you.

  • Goal: a Table folder for each table in TABLES: from its query, when QUERIES gives one, its logic written for you; else from a Template, its logic for you to write.
  • Action: Run the cell below. If QUERIES gives a query, each table it reads that has no Table reference gets one first, from its DESCRIBE through your query, its Key proposed, as the references Workflow writes one: it names each file it added. Each query is converted as the convert Workflow converts one: in a batch of its own, named for its table, in admin/workflows/convert/; the batch moves to finished/ once compared.
  • Done when each table in TABLES has its Table folder in tables/; then step 2.

Code cell 6

project.new_table(TABLES, queries=QUERIES, query=query)

It stops with this message

TypeError:
  What happened:  project.new_table was given an empty list, where a name or a list of names goes.
  Why it matters: It does its job for each name, in turn: one name as text, or several in a list.
  Usual fix:      Give project.new_table one name, as text in quotes, or a list of names in square brackets, each in quotes.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

2. Write each table's logic in its Table notebook, and save it

It is a decision, yours to make.

  • Goal: logic in each Table notebook that loads, so its preview() builds.
  • Action: For each table in TABLES, open its Table notebook, in its Table folder. If QUERIES gives the table's query, its logic is written already, from that query: read its logic cell, the one whose first line is # logic, and each # CONVERSION: line in it, which project.check() lists too, and change nothing unless you want to, since step 8 compares its rows with the query's. If QUERIES gives it no query, fill in each <...> in its code cells as its text cell says, and save it. Then run the cell below: it says what stops each table's logic, if anything does.
  • Done when the cell below runs with no error; then step 3.

If it says it kept a Table reference, as it does for a table made from its query, that file is yours already, its columns written. Either way, open each Table reference it names, fill in each TODO line it names by hand, such as one line saying what one row is, and save the file, now or when you like: no step waits on them.

Its report lists each day not loaded, with the warehouse's error, and each that wrote no rows. A Rebuilt table has no Seed, and running the cell again loads nothing twice. Then project.run_tables sends each table's write(), through your execute.

admin/workflows/promote/promote.ipynb

promote

Carries Saved tables from your Dev Database to your Prod Database.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.
  • Prod Database: the database others read your finished Saved tables from, which a table reaches only once promoted from the Dev Database: PROD in admin/databases.py.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Table folder: the folder in tables/ that holds everything about one Saved table: its Table notebook, its past lineage, and its Hive.
  • Original: the table a converted script writes, in the warehouse already, which its Saved table copies; for a query alone, such as one Copilot wrote from a workbook, the rows the query gives.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Reference sheet: references/reference_sheet.csv, one table listing every column of every table the Project has a Table reference for.
  • Database module: a file such as references/ops.py, which gives each table of one database by name, such as ops.jobs.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Table notebook: the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create, preview and write its Saved table; its logic cell is the one whose first line is # logic.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.

Before you start

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so does project.check(), which names the next step.

Decision rules

  • If you want the steps run for you, as most do: fill in the names cell and save the notebook, run "Run up to your Decisions", paste what it prints into the decisions cell under it, fill it in and save, then run "Run the rest". Each runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped.
  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell under "Step by step", in turn, in order.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the last cell. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Each dev table in TABLES has a prod copy: its Table folder in tables/ and its Table reference in references/.
  • Each prod copy's Table reference has its location= and leaves no Decision open.
  • admin/sent.csv records each prod copy created and written, and each History table seeded first.
  • references/reference_sheet.csv lists each prod copy's columns.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[2]))  # the Project: three up

from sqlglot_composer import admin, hive_settings, project, run_execute, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import connect, query, execute

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

admin/workflows/references/references.ipynb

references

Writes a Table reference for each table your Project reads, from its DESCRIBE.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Reference sheet: references/reference_sheet.csv, one table listing every column of every table the Project has a Table reference for.
  • Database module: a file such as references/ops.py, which gives each table of one database by name, such as ops.jobs.

Before you start

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so does project.check(), which names the next step.

Each step's own cell, to run or run again one step on purpose; the cells above run them all for you.

If no Key was proposed for a table, or its verdict says it repeats, then write its Key in place of its <FILL IN>, as a list such as ["run_id"], or None when no columns name one row; how-to 21 shows how to check one with check_key first.

admin/workflows/schedule/schedule.ipynb

schedule

Makes a Scheduled run, a notebook an outside scheduler runs top to bottom, from admin/templates/scheduled_run.py: you fill in its cells in its own notebook, in scheduled_runs/.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Scheduled run: a notebook in scheduled_runs/<name>/ that an outside scheduler, such as papermill, runs top to bottom: it writes the tables it needs, in order, and gives back what you asked for.
  • Statement: a query your code builds, which becomes one Hive string.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.

Before step 1

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so do project.check(), which names the next step, and project.do_next(), the cell before the last.

Decision rules

  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell in turn, in order.
  • If you want them run for you, run the last cell, project.run_workflow(...), in place of their cells. It runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped: go on from that step.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the cell before the last. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • scheduled_runs/<name>/<name>.ipynb is there for each name in NAME.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

  • Goal: the notebook of the Scheduled run NAME names, scheduled_runs/<name>/<name>.ipynb, made from admin/templates/scheduled_run.py and registered.
  • Action: Run the cell below. It refuses a name already taken, or one Python can't import. Once the run's notebook is there, this step is done, and running the cell again is refused, as the name is taken. Then open the run's notebook and fill in its <...>: its parameters, its connection and the Statements it runs, as its own text cell says.

admin/workflows/upgrade/upgrade.ipynb

upgrade

Carries the Project's files to the Toolbox you copied in, once you have restarted the kernel.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Upgrade: the Project action that carries a Project's files from the Toolbox version that last wrote them to the one copied in, one version at a time.
  • UPGRADE mark: a # UPGRADE comment the Upgrade puts at a line it leaves for you to change.
  • Prod Database: the database others read your finished Saved tables from, which a table reaches only once promoted from the Dev Database: PROD in admin/databases.py.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Project action: a one-time change to a Project's files, run from the admin folder or a Workflow's notebook, and recorded in admin/admin_log.md.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.

Decision rules

  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell in turn, in order.
  • If you want them run for you, run the last cell, project.run_workflow(...), in place of their cells. It runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped: go on from that step.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • admin/project_version.txt names the Toolbox version you copied in.
  • No # UPGRADE mark is left in the Project's own files.
  • project.check() lists no UPGRADE mark and no upgrade to make.

building_blocks/runs_per_job.py

Show its 41 lines
"""runs_per_job - each job's runs, failed runs and minutes for each dt it reads.

Why: more than one Statement needs each job's runs, failed runs and minutes, such as each
team's failed runs and a check of one day, and writing them once here means they can never
be counted two different ways.

Every run counts, TEST runs and runs still going among them, since each one takes the
cluster's time: a team that wants TEST runs left out says so in its own Statement.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Condition, Table, count_rows, derived, statement,
    sum_of,
)
from references.ops__job_runs import failed, job_runs, minutes  # failed, minutes: Aliases


def runs_per_job(reads: Condition) -> Table:
    """One row per job for each dt of the rows `reads` picks: its runs, failed runs and
    minutes.

    `reads` is a condition on ops.job_runs, such as equals(job_runs.dt, "2026-09-24"):
    the table is partitioned by dt, so it must filter dt. dt is kept in GROUP_BY, so the
    counts of each dt stay apart when `reads` picks several, and in SELECT, so a Statement
    that reads this can tell its days apart, or keep them apart in its own GROUP_BY.
    """
    return derived(
        "runs_per_job",
        statement(
            SELECT(
                job_runs.job_id,
                job_runs.dt,
                AS(count_rows(), "runs"),
                AS(count_rows(where=failed), "failed_runs"),
                AS(sum_of(minutes), "minutes"),
            ),
            FROM(job_runs),
            WHERE(reads),
            GROUP_BY(job_runs.job_id, job_runs.dt),
        ),
    )

references/mart.py

Show its 41 lines
"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.alerts_per_job.job_id.

Its tables: alerts_per_job (references/mart__alerts_per_job.py), alerts_per_severity
(references/mart__alerts_per_severity.py), daily_job_runs (references/mart__daily_job_runs.py),
team_day (references/mart__team_day.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__alerts_per_job import alerts_per_job
from references.mart__alerts_per_severity import alerts_per_severity
from references.mart__daily_job_runs import daily_job_runs
from references.mart__team_day import team_day

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["alerts_per_job", "alerts_per_severity", "daily_job_runs", "team_day"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

references/mart__alerts_per_job.py

Show its 42 lines
"""mart.alerts_per_job - one row per job and day with alerts: alerts, high alerts.

Why: its notebook saves these numbers once a day, so mart.team_day and anyone else can read
them without joining every alert to its run again.

A job whose runs raised no alert that day has no row.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py; it was
filled in by hand, since the table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

alerts_per_job = Table(
    "mart.alerts_per_job",
    columns={
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "alerts": "bigint",
        "high_alerts": "bigint",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["job_id", "dt"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/alerts_per_job",  # its own folder
    descriptions={
        "alerts": "every alert the job's runs raised that day",
        "high_alerts": "those whose severity is high",
        "dt": "the day the alerts were raised, as yyyy-MM-dd",
    },
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table, such as:
#     from references.mart__alerts_per_job import alerts_per_job, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = alerts_per_job.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.
#     <alias> = equals(alerts_per_job.<column>, <value>)

references/ops__job_runs.py

Show its 43 lines
"""ops.job_runs - one row per run of a job, on the day it ran.

Why: the project's notebooks read the table through this file, so each column they name, and the
key each join and count relies on, where it has one, is checked as the Statement is built.

project.add_references wrote this file from the table's DESCRIBE, its partition columns, if any,
among it. Its three TODO lines were then filled in by hand: this docstring's first line, the
key, and does_not_add_up.
"""
from sqlglot_composer import Table, equals

job_runs = Table(
    "ops.job_runs",
    columns={
        "run_id": "bigint",
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "status": "string",
        "duration_mins": "int",
        "avg_retry_secs": "double",
        "dt": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["dt"],
    key=["run_id"],  # filled in by hand
    # filled in by hand: summing these gives a wrong total, since avg_retry_secs is an average
    does_not_add_up=["avg_retry_secs"],
    descriptions={
        "status": "SUCCESS / FAILED / TEST, NULL while running",
    },
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table: from references.ops__job_runs import job_runs, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = job_runs.status
