admin/templates/scheduled_run.py

Show its 117 lines
# %% [markdown]
# # Scheduled run: <NAME>
#
# Fill in every `<...>` in the code cells below, brackets and all: the notebook won't run with
# one left. They are:
#   - `<RESULT_NAME>`: the name a SELECT's rows come back by, in quotes, such as
#     `"week_totals"`;
#   - `<SAVED_TABLE>`: a Saved table whose Table notebook's logic it uses, such as
#     `mart.team_days`;
#   - `<LOGIC_FUNCTION>`: the function of that logic it calls, with what it takes, such as
#     `week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY)`, two parameters, or `write()`.
#
# Follow the steps in order, as written.
#
# ## Terms
#
# - **Scheduled run**: this notebook, which an outside scheduler runs top to bottom: building
#   the Statements and their Hive in the Build cell, then sending them in the Run cell.
# - **Parameter**: a value the scheduler passes in, one of the names in the `# parameters`
#   cell, read by its type hint: `"2026-11-01"` given for a `datetime.date` is that date.
# - **Run value**: what a Python function gives each time a Statement is built, such as the
#   latest Partition of a table; `run_values=` gives one a value of yours for this run.
#
# ## Steps
#
# 1. **Name the parameters.** Goal: each value the scheduler passes in. Action: write each in
#    the `# parameters` cell as a name, its type hint and its default, such as
#    `RUN_DATE: datetime.date | None = None`; keep `START_FROM`. Done when each has a hint;
#    then step 2.
# 2. **Connect.** Goal: this run's account. Action: in the setup cell, write your connection
#    in the `connect(...)` line and delete its `#`. Done when the setup cell runs; then step 3.
# 3. **Name the Statements.** Goal: what this run sends. Action: write each in the dict
#    `statements()` gives, in the `# scheduled` cell, by name. If it is a Table notebook's
#    logic, load it: `project.load("mart.team_days").week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY)`. If
#    it is your own, compose it there, with `statement(...)`. Done when each is named; then
#    step 4.
# 4. **Build.** Goal: the Hive, in order, to read before anything is sent. Action: run the
#    cells down to the Build cell. If a table it would write is written elsewhere, name it in
#    `leave_out=`. Done when it prints each Statement's Hive in order; then step 5.
# 5. **Run.** Goal: the Statements sent and the SELECTs' rows back. Action: run the Run cell,
#    or have the scheduler run the notebook. Done when it ends with no error; each SELECT's
#    rows are in `results` by the name `statements()` gives it, such as
#    `results["week_totals"]`.
#
# ## What the build prints
#
# First each parameter as used, then each Statement in the order it is sent, headed like
# `-- 3 of 7: mart.team_days (read by week_totals)`, and its Hive. The writes of each table of
# this Project the Statements read are added, all the way up, before what reads them.
#
# Made by `project.new_scheduled_run("<NAME>")` from `admin/templates/scheduled_run.py`.
#
# ## Self-check
#
# - No code cell holds a `<...>`.
# - Each parameter in the `# parameters` cell has a type hint.
# - The Build cell prints each Statement's Hive, in order, and sends nothing.

# %%
# parameters
# The values a scheduler passes in, each a name, its type hint and its default. A scheduler
# passes text, which the Build cell reads by the hint, and refuses a value it can't read. The
# hints it reads: datetime.date, int, float, bool, str, each alone, in list[...] or with
# | None, such as list[datetime.date] for "2026-11-01,2026-11-08".
import datetime  # for a datetime.date hint

START_FROM: str = ""  # a table or SELECT to start from, as a failed run prints it; "" sends all
# RUN_DATE: datetime.date | None = None  # such as "2026-11-01", passed in as text

# %%
# Setup: the Project on Python's path, the Toolbox and your connection
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import Statement, project, statement
from warehouse import connect, execute, query

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
# connect(your_connection)

# %%
# scheduled
# The Statements this run sends, each by its name: a SELECT's rows come back in results by it.
# A Table notebook's logic gives them through project.load, such as
#     "week_totals": project.load("mart.team_days").week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY),
# and a Statement of your own is composed here with statement(...). A value worked out each
# time the run is built goes in as run_value(function), a function of this cell, such as
# week_totals(run_value(week_start), ...): import run_value from sqlglot_composer here.


def statements() -> dict[str, Statement]:
    """What this run sends, by name: the build adds the writes of each table they read."""
    return {
        "<RESULT_NAME>": project.load("<SAVED_TABLE>").<LOGIC_FUNCTION>,
    }

# %%
# Build: the Statements, in order, and their Hive, printed; nothing sent
# It reads the parameters first, then builds each Statement, adding the writes of each table
# of this Project they read. run_values={"period_end": RUN_DATE} gives a parameter of yours,
# RUN_DATE, to each Run value whose function is named period_end, in the writes added too;
# leave_out=["mart.daily_job_runs"] leaves out that table's write, and what only it reads.
built = project.build_run(statements, run_values={}, leave_out=[])

# %%
# Run: each one sent, in the build's order
# Each write is sent through execute and each SELECT through query; each SELECT's rows are in
# results by the name statements() gives it, such as results["week_totals"]. One that fails
# stops the rest, and the run prints the START_FROM to send the rest from.
results = built.run(query, execute)

admin/workflows/change_a_saved_table/change_a_saved_table.ipynb

change_a_saved_table

Changes a Saved table in the warehouse with one of the admin module's Admin statements, each logged in admin/admin_log.md.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Admin statement: a Statement from the Toolbox's admin module that changes a Saved table's definition or deletes its data, such as dropping some of its Partitions; it shows what it will change before its Hive.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.
  • Statement: a query your code builds, which becomes one Hive string.

Before step 1

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so do project.check(), which names the next step, and project.do_next(), the cell before the last.

Decision rules

  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell in turn, in order.
  • If you want them run for you, run the last cell, project.run_workflow(...), in place of their cells. It runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped: go on from that step.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the cell before the last. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Step 1's cell sets change with its arguments, no ... left.
  • admin/sent.csv records the Admin statement on TABLE, and admin/admin_log.md logs it.
  • You ran the check its Check first line names, and any check after it.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

admin/workflows/convert/convert.ipynb

convert

Turns Hive someone else wrote, one query or a batch of scripts, into your own code: each table the scripts write becomes a Saved table in your Dev Database, with its Table folder, and what only you can decide of it, a Decision, answered in the decisions cell.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.
  • Batch: the scripts converted together, under one name.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.
  • Table folder: the folder in tables/ that holds everything about one Saved table: its Table notebook, its past lineage, and its Hive.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Original: the table a converted script writes, in the warehouse already, which its Saved table copies; for a query alone, such as one Copilot wrote from a workbook, the rows the query gives.
  • Table notebook: the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create, preview and write its Saved table; its logic cell is the one whose first line is # logic.

Before you start

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so does project.check(), which names the next step.

Decision rules

  • If you want the steps run for you, as most do: fill in the names cell and save the notebook, run "Run up to your Decisions", paste what it prints into the decisions cell under it, fill it in and save, then run "Run the rest". Each runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped.
  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell under "Step by step", in turn, in order.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the last cell. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Each table the scripts write has its Table folder in tables/ and its Table reference in references/.
  • Each table's conversion/checklist.md says None under Open Decisions.
  • admin/sent.csv records each table created and written, and each History table seeded first.
  • Each table's conversion/compare.md says it passed, and the batch's folder is in admin/workflows/convert/finished/.
  • project.check() names no next step of this batch, and names it under Done.

FILES = []  # the scripts' files, such as ["daily.hql"], or an Excel workbook's, such as ["runs.xlsx"], each calculation of it a Saved table, each beside this notebook or a path anywhere, such as "C:/work/daily.hql", read where it is: left [], every .hql file beside this notebook, never a workbook, which you name here; or HIVE when it is given

Step by step

1. Convert the scripts: each table they write a Saved table in dev

  • Goal: a Table folder and a Table reference for each table the scripts write, and for each calculation of a workbook that nothing else in it reads, each a Saved table in your Dev Database.

ValueError:
  What happened:  project.convert("", ...): '' isn't a plain lowercase name.
  Why it matters: A batch's folder is named by it, and a query alone's Saved table, in your Dev Database, apart from a table's name, which holds a dot.
  Usual fix:      Use lowercase letters, digits and _, starting with a letter, such as "team_runs".
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

2. Answer each table's open Decisions in the decisions cell

It is a decision, yours to make.

  • Goal: an answer to each open Decision of the batch's tables, each Script tag's value among them.
  • Action: Run the cell below, project.check(): it names each open Decision and prints a whole DECISIONS to paste over the decisions cell's; then write each answer in the decisions cell, DECISIONS, by the table's name as project.check() writes it, each <FILL IN> given its answer, then save the notebook. Each table's conversion/checklist.md, in its Table folder, lists its open Decisions. A Script tag, a <PARM="NAME"/> the program that ran the scripts, such as a scheduler, replaced with a value before Hive read it, is answered under "tags" with that value as Hive. A tag standing alone, as in dt <= <PARM="PERIOD_END"/>, is answered with the value's own quotes: {"tags": {"PERIOD_END": "'2026-10-31'"}}. A tag already inside quotes, as in '<PARM="SKIP_TEAM"/>', is answered without them: {"tags": {"SKIP_TEAM": "nobody"}}. To change one, answer it again and run steps 3, 5 and 6 again: the table is made already, so 4 stays done.
  • Done when the decisions cell answers each Decision project.check() names for this batch; then step 3.

If you want other Partitions compared too, each one your dev table holds as well as its original, then list them in PARTITIONS in the names cell, such as [{"dt": "2026-09-23"}], each value with no quotes of its own, run the names cell, and run this step's cell again: each table partitioned by dt is compared on its latest and on each listed, the others on their latest, each result its own section, kept beside the ones before; a result that failed is replaced when that Partition is compared again. If the batch is finished already, then the same: list them in PARTITIONS and run this step's cell, or "Run the rest", which compares a finished batch again whenever PARTITIONS lists a Partition, and says the batch was finished.

# Batch job_hours

admin/workflows/convert/job_hours.xlsx, a workbook read where it is, its final kept as its Hive in scripts/: job_hours.hql. Each table they write is a Saved table in the Dev Database, mart; one they only create has its Table reference alone.

## Tables

- mart.job_hours, from its query: tables/mart__job_hours/, its checklist tables/mart__job_hours/conversion/checklist.md
  - # CONVERSION: FROM(job_events, reads_all_partitions=True), a note on its checklist
  - # CONVERSION: LEFT_JOIN(o, reads_all_partitions=True), a note on its checklist
  - # CONVERSION: sum_of(run_hours.hours, adds_up=True), a note on its checklist

## The Workbook check of job_hours.xlsx

It ran the Hive of each table the workbook gave, on this computer, over the workbook's own Tables, and compared its rows with the values the workbook shows, as last saved. A difference means the Hive doesn't give what the workbook's formula, query or PivotTable gave on the same rows: look at each one, though none stops anything. The workbook's rows are an old sample, so the warehouse's rows will differ from them: this compares the Hive with the workbook alone, never with the warehouse.

- mart.job_hours, from PivotTable "PivotTable1": every cell matches the workbook, 2 cells compared.

## Each statement, in the order they run

1. job_hours.hql line 1, `WITH job_events AS ( SELECT job_id, event_type, dt, minutes / 60 AS hours FRO...`: mart.job_hours, written by its notebook

admin/workflows/convert/finished/job_hours/scripts/job_hours.hql

Show its 28 lines
WITH job_events AS (
  SELECT
    job_id,
    event_type,
    dt,
    minutes / 60 AS hours
  FROM ops.job_events
), run_hours AS (
  SELECT
    e.job_id,
    o.team,
    e.hours
  FROM job_events AS e
  LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS o
    ON e.job_id = o.job_id AND e.dt = o.dt
  WHERE
    e.event_type = 'finish'
)
SELECT
  job_id,
  SUM(hours) AS sum_of_hours
FROM run_hours
WHERE
  (
    team = 'data'
  ) /* Pivot filter: slicer team */
GROUP BY
  job_id;

# Batch team_failures

admin/workflows/convert/job_hours.xlsx

An Excel workbook, shown as its text, as python copilot/workbook_text.py writes it for a Copilot chat.

Show its 190 lines
Sheet "Steps": the empty Formula workbook's steps, as it ships.
Sheet "Parameters"
  Excel Table "Parameters", A1:B2
    Headers: name | value
    Row 2:  | 
  D1: Parameters: the fixed values your formulas use, one row per value. Put its name under name and the value under value, such as tax_rate and 0.2 (a plain number, not 20%), then read it by name in a formula: XLOOKUP("tax_rate", Parameters[name], Parameters[value]). Leave the Table empty if no formula uses a fixed value. See step 6 on the Steps sheet.
Sheet "Keys"
  Excel Table "Keys", A1:B2
    Headers: table | key_columns
    Row 2:  | 
  D1: Keys: only for a Table where a value was typed over a formula. Add one row for that Table, however many values were typed over in it: the Table's name under table, and under key_columns the typed column or columns that tell its rows apart, separated by commas, such as job_runs with run_id, or sales with region, month. Copilot uses them to find the row a typed value belongs to. If no column tells the rows apart, leave the Table out. A Table with no value typed over a formula needs no row here. See step 10 on the Steps sheet.

Power Query "run_hours" (loads into Excel Table "run_hours"):
    let
        Source = Excel.CurrentWorkbook(){[Name="job_events"]}[Content],
        Owners = Excel.CurrentWorkbook(){[Name="job_owners"]}[Content],
        Finished = Table.SelectRows(Source, each [event_type] = "finish"),
        Joined = Table.NestedJoin(Finished, {"job_id", "dt"}, Owners, {"job_id", "dt"}, "owner",
            JoinKind.LeftOuter),
        Expanded = Table.ExpandTableColumn(Joined, "owner", {"team"}),
        Kept = Table.SelectColumns(Expanded, {"job_id", "team", "hours", "dt"})
    in
        Kept
Order, each piece after every piece it reads:
  1. Excel Table "Parameters"
  2. Excel Table "Keys"
  3. Excel Table "job_events"
  4. Excel Table "job_owners"
  5. Power Query "run_hours", reads Excel Table "job_events", Excel Table "job_owners"
  6. Excel Table "run_hours", loaded by Power Query "run_hours"
  7. PivotTable "PivotTable1", reads Excel Table "run_hours"
Each final's query reads the steps it needs as CTEs in this order, each CTE after every CTE it reads.

admin/workflows/convert/team_failures.hql

Show its 21 lines
-- Events and failures per team and day, as the ops team wrote it
WITH ev AS (
  SELECT e.job_id, e.dt, e.event_type
  FROM ops.job_events e
  WHERE e.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-20' AND '2026-09-24'
    AND e.event_type IN ('start', 'retry', 'fail', 'finish')
), per_team AS (
  SELECT o.team, ev.dt,
         COUNT(*) AS events,
         SUM(CASE WHEN ev.event_type = 'fail' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS fails
  FROM ev
  JOIN ops.job_owners o ON o.job_id = ev.job_id AND o.dt = ev.dt
  GROUP BY o.team, ev.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE ops_reports.team_failures PARTITION (dt)
SELECT p.team,
       CASE p.team WHEN 'data' THEN 'Data platform' WHEN 'web' THEN 'Web' ELSE 'Other' END AS team_name,
       p.events,
       p.fails,
       p.dt
FROM per_team p

admin/workflows/new_table/new_table.ipynb

new_table

Makes a Table folder for each new Saved table: from its query, when QUERIES gives one, its logic written for you; else from a Template, its logic for you to write. A Building block, a piece several notebooks share, is made with project.new_block(...).

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Table folder: the folder in tables/ that holds everything about one Saved table: its Table notebook, its past lineage, and its Hive.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Template: the empty shape of a Table notebook, in admin/templates/, each specific left as a <...> to fill in.
  • Building block: a piece of your own code several notebooks share, a file of its own in building_blocks/.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Batch: the scripts converted together, under one name.
  • Table notebook: the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create, preview and write its Saved table; its logic cell is the one whose first line is # logic.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Original: the table a converted script writes, in the warehouse already, which its Saved table copies; for a query alone, such as one Copilot wrote from a workbook, the rows the query gives.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.

Before you start

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so does project.check(), which names the next step.

Decision rules

  • If you want the steps run for you, as most do: fill in the names cell and save the notebook, run "Run up to your Decisions", paste what it prints into the decisions cell under it, fill it in and save, then run "Run the rest". Each runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped.
  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell under "Step by step", in turn, in order.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the last cell. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Each table in TABLES has its Table folder in tables/, and its step 2 cell runs with no error.
  • Each table's Table reference lists its columns and leaves no Decision open.
  • admin/sent.csv records each table created and written.
  • Each table made from a query has a conversion/compare.md that says it passed.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[2]))  # the Project: three up

from sqlglot_composer import admin, hive_settings, project, run_execute, run_query, show_hive

# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

At a Decision, it prints a whole DECISIONS: paste it into the decisions cell under it, fill in each <FILL IN>, and save the notebook. With nothing waiting on you, it runs every step to the end, its calls to the warehouse too: give it dry_run=True first to see what it would send.

Code cell 4

DECISIONS = {}  # your answer to each open Decision, by the table's name as
# project.check() writes it, such as
# {"mart_dev.weekly_ops": {"key": ["job_id"], "types": {"fails": "bigint"},
#                          "tags": {"PERIOD_END": "'2026-10-31'"}}}
# "key": None says no columns name one row. project.check() names each one open, and
# prints a whole DECISIONS to paste over this line: fill in each <FILL IN>. A Key
# convert or new_table proposed, marked # proposed, is kept unless you answer "key"
# here; one add_references proposed stays open until you accept it.
# "key": "proposed" accepts either as if typed. "all_tables" answers for every table
# of this notebook, and a table's own answer overwrites it, kind by kind and tag by
# tag:
# {"all_tables": {"key": "proposed",
#                 "tags": {"PERIOD_END": "'2026-10-31'"}}}
# accepts each table's proposed Key and gives each table holding PERIOD_END that
# value. A table's location is placed under "location_root" in
# admin/databases.py; answer "location" only for one it can't place.
# "kind" says whether each write replaces the whole table, "rebuilt", or keeps the
# rows already there, "history", as a daily table keeps each day: "proposed" accepts
# the kind proposed from its write, which project.check() names, and under
# "all_tables" accepts each table's.
# A tag may be answered with a Run value's function of your Project's, imported above
# DECISIONS, such as "PERIOD_END": period_end. For a table's latest Partition, write
# project.new_run_value("period_end", latest_partition="ops.job_runs.dt") at the top
# of this cell, the import it prints under it, and run the cell once: it writes the
# function into building_blocks/run_values.py. And "seed", such as
# {"dt": {"from": "2026-01-01"}}, limits the Partitions a table that reads itself
# copies: how-to 09 shows both.

It shows nothing.

Step by step

Each step's own cell, to run or run again one step on purpose; the cells above run them all for you.

1. Make each Table folder, converting each table's query QUERIES gives

It changes only files, so project.do_next() runs it for you.

  • Goal: a Table folder for each table in TABLES: from its query, when QUERIES gives one, its logic written for you; else from a Template, its logic for you to write.
  • Action: Run the cell below. If QUERIES gives a query, each table it reads that has no Table reference gets one first, from its DESCRIBE through your query, its Key proposed, as the references Workflow writes one: it names each file it added. Each query is converted as the convert Workflow converts one: in a batch of its own, named for its table, in admin/workflows/convert/; the batch moves to finished/ once compared.
  • Done when each table in TABLES has its Table folder in tables/; then step 2.

Code cell 6

project.new_table(TABLES, queries=QUERIES, query=query)

It stops with this message

TypeError:
  What happened:  project.new_table was given an empty list, where a name or a list of names goes.
  Why it matters: It does its job for each name, in turn: one name as text, or several in a list.
  Usual fix:      Give project.new_table one name, as text in quotes, or a list of names in square brackets, each in quotes.
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

2. Write each table's logic in its Table notebook, and save it

It is a decision, yours to make.

  • Goal: logic in each Table notebook that loads, so its preview() builds.
  • Action: For each table in TABLES, open its Table notebook, in its Table folder. If QUERIES gives the table's query, its logic is written already, from that query: read its logic cell, the one whose first line is # logic, and each # CONVERSION: line in it, which project.check() lists too, and change nothing unless you want to, since step 8 compares its rows with the query's. If QUERIES gives it no query, fill in each <...> in its code cells as its text cell says, and save it. Then run the cell below: it says what stops each table's logic, if anything does.
  • Done when the cell below runs with no error; then step 3.

Code cell 7

for table in TABLES:
    project.load(table).preview()  # says what stops it, if anything does

Not run: a cell above stopped, as Run All stops.

3. Write each Table reference from its logic

It changes only files, so project.do_next() runs it for you.

  • Goal: each table's Table reference written from its logic: its columns and types from preview(), its partition columns from PARTITIONED_BY, and the Key its logic makes.
  • Action: Run the cell below. It names each TODO line each Table reference leaves, such as its docstring's first line, which waits on nothing. If it says it kept a Table reference, as it does for a table made from its query, that file is yours already, its columns written. Either way, open each Table reference it names, fill in each TODO line it names by hand, such as one line saying what one row is, and save the file, now or when you like: no step waits on them.
  • Done when each table's Table reference in references/ lists its columns, no longer the stand-in new_table wrote; then step 4.

Code cell 8

project.reference_from_logic(TABLES, query=query)

Not run: a cell above stopped, as Run All stops.

It is a decision, yours to make.

  • Goal: an answer to each open Decision of the new tables.
  • Action: Run the cell below, project.check(): it names each open Decision and prints a whole DECISIONS to paste over the decisions cell's; then write each answer in the decisions cell, DECISIONS, by the table's name as project.check() writes it, then save the notebook.
  • Done when the decisions cell answers each Decision project.check() names for these tables; then step 5.

  • Action: Run the cell below. It prints the rows each table's preview() gives, read through your query. project.check() names this step beside step 7.
  • Done when you have read each table's rows, and they are the rows it should hold; then step 7.

7. Create and write each in dev

It is a call to the warehouse, yours to run.

  • Goal: each table made and written in your Dev Database, each History table's past rows loaded first, its Seed.
  • Action: Run the cell below. It sends each table's create(), through your execute. project.seed_tables first loads each History table's past rows, its Seed, once, while it holds no Partitions yet, through your query and execute: copied from its original, or, for a table with none, by running its write as of each past day, as SEED in its Table reference picks them. Its report lists each day not loaded, with the warehouse's error, and each that wrote no rows. A Rebuilt table has no Seed, and running the cell again loads nothing twice. Then project.run_tables sends each table's write(), through your execute.
  • Done when admin/sent.csv records each table written; then step 8.

admin/workflows/promote/promote.ipynb

promote

Carries Saved tables from your Dev Database to your Prod Database.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Decisions cell: DECISIONS, the code cell after the names cell, where you answer each open Decision a step waits on, by the table's name.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.
  • Prod Database: the database others read your finished Saved tables from, which a table reaches only once promoted from the Dev Database: PROD in admin/databases.py.
  • Decision: one thing about one table that only you decide, such as its location or its Key; the table's files say whether it is answered yet.
  • Table reference: a script in references/ describing one table: its columns and their types, its Partitions and its Key.
  • Table folder: the folder in tables/ that holds everything about one Saved table: its Table notebook, its past lineage, and its Hive.
  • Original: the table a converted script writes, in the warehouse already, which its Saved table copies; for a query alone, such as one Copilot wrote from a workbook, the rows the query gives.
  • Partition: the rows of a table that share one value of each column it is partitioned by.
  • Reference sheet: references/reference_sheet.csv, one table listing every column of every table the Project has a Table reference for.
  • Database module: a file such as references/ops.py, which gives each table of one database by name, such as ops.jobs.
  • Key: the columns whose values name exactly one row of a table.
  • Table notebook: the notebook in a Table folder whose cells create, preview and write its Saved table; its logic cell is the one whose first line is # logic.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.

Before you start

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so does project.check(), which names the next step.

Decision rules

  • If you want the steps run for you, as most do: fill in the names cell and save the notebook, run "Run up to your Decisions", paste what it prints into the decisions cell under it, fill it in and save, then run "Run the rest". Each runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped.
  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell under "Step by step", in turn, in order.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the last cell. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • Each dev table in TABLES has a prod copy: its Table folder in tables/ and its Table reference in references/.
  • Each prod copy's Table reference has its location= and leaves no Decision open.
  • admin/sent.csv records each prod copy created and written, and each History table seeded first.
  • references/reference_sheet.csv lists each prod copy's columns.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

Code cell 1

import pathlib
import sys

from sqlglot_composer import admin, hive_settings, project, run_execute, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import connect, query, execute

# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

Step by step

Each step's own cell, to run or run again one step on purpose; the cells above run them all for you.

1. Write the Table references from DESCRIBE

It is a call to the warehouse, yours to run.

  • Goal: a Table reference for each table in TABLES, its columns and their types read from its DESCRIBE, and its Key proposed and checked.
  • Action: Run the cell below. It reads each table's DESCRIBE, counts the distinct values of each id column on its latest Partition, and checks the Key it proposes with check_key, all through your query, and writes only files.
  • Done when each table in TABLES has its Table reference in references/; then step 2.

Code cell 6

project.add_references(TABLES, query=query)

It stops with this message

ValueError:
  What happened:  project.add_references([], query=...): names isn't a list of tables.
  Why it matters: It writes a Table reference for each table in the list.
  Usual fix:      Write it as a list, such as project.add_references(["ops.jobs"], query=query).
  Opt-out:        none - this one can't be switched off.

2. Answer each table's open Decisions in the decisions cell

It is a decision, yours to make.

  • Goal: an answer to each table's open Key: the Key step 1 proposed, accepted, or the columns whose values name exactly one row, or None when no columns do.
  • Action: Run the cell below, project.check(): it names each open Decision and prints a whole DECISIONS to paste over the decisions cell's; then fill in each <FILL IN> in it, if any, and save the notebook. Step 1 proposed a Key for each table it could, from its columns and a count of each id column's distinct values, and checked it with check_key, printing each verdict. A proposed Key is pasted as it is, which accepts it; for two tables or more, "all_tables": {"key": "proposed"} accepts each. If no Key was proposed for a table, or its verdict says it repeats, then write its Key in place of its <FILL IN>, as a list such as ["run_id"], or None when no columns name one row; how-to 21 shows how to check one with check_key first.
  • Done when the decisions cell answers each Key project.check() names for these tables; then step 3.

project.check()

Not run: a cell above stopped, as Run All stops.

3. Write each answer into its table's Table reference

It changes only files, so project.do_next() runs it for you.

  • Goal: each answer written into its table's Table reference.
  • Action: Run the cell below. Step 1 proposed each Table reference's first line, what one row is, from its Key, and its does_not_add_up=, the columns whose names say their values can't be added up across rows, such as an average, each marked # proposed; writing a Key proposes its first line again. Read them, and change any that is wrong, such as a distinct count no name gives away. The cell names each TODO line left, as a table with no Key keeps its first line's; no step waits on them, so fill each in when you like.
  • Done when each table's Table reference leaves no Key open; then step 4.

Code cell 8

project.write_decisions(DECISIONS, tables=TABLES)

Not run: a cell above stopped, as Run All stops.

4. Refresh the Reference sheet and the Database modules

It changes only files, so project.do_next() runs it for you.

  • Goal: each table in the Reference sheet and in its Database module.
  • Action: Run the cell below. If you change a Table reference after it, such as filling in a TODO line, then run it again: project.check() names this step again, saying which Table reference changed.
  • Done when references/reference_sheet.csv is written again after each table's Table reference last changed; the Workflow is done: go through the Self-check at the top.

project.refresh_reference_sheet(TABLES)

Not run: a cell above stopped, as Run All stops.

admin/workflows/schedule/schedule.ipynb

schedule

Makes a Scheduled run, a notebook an outside scheduler runs top to bottom, from admin/templates/scheduled_run.py: you fill in its cells in its own notebook, in scheduled_runs/.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Names cell: the code cell after the one that finds the Project. It holds this Workflow's inputs, the start materials you fill in once, each with what to write in its comment; each step's call reads them.
  • Scheduled run: a notebook in scheduled_runs/<name>/ that an outside scheduler, such as papermill, runs top to bottom: it writes the tables it needs, in order, and gives back what you asked for.
  • Statement: a query your code builds, which becomes one Hive string.
  • Hive: the SQL your warehouse runs.

Before step 1

Fill in the names cell below and save the notebook: each call reads it, and so do project.check(), which names the next step, and project.do_next(), the cell before the last.

Decision rules

  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell in turn, in order.
  • If you want them run for you, run the last cell, project.run_workflow(...), in place of their cells. It runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped: go on from that step.
  • If you want only the steps that change files run for you, run project.do_next(), the cell before the last. It runs them up to the first it can't.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • scheduled_runs/<name>/<name>.ipynb is there for each name in NAME.
  • project.check() names no next step of this Workflow.

Code cell 1

# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

It changes only files, so project.do_next() runs it for you.

  • Goal: the notebook of the Scheduled run NAME names, scheduled_runs/<name>/<name>.ipynb, made from admin/templates/scheduled_run.py and registered.
  • Action: Run the cell below. It refuses a name already taken, or one Python can't import. Once the run's notebook is there, this step is done, and running the cell again is refused, as the name is taken. Then open the run's notebook and fill in its <...>: its parameters, its connection and the Statements it runs, as its own text cell says.
  • Done when scheduled_runs/<name>/<name>.ipynb is there; the Workflow is done: go through the Self-check at the top.

admin/workflows/upgrade/upgrade.ipynb

upgrade

Carries the Project's files to the Toolbox you copied in, once you have restarted the kernel.

Terms

  • Step: each numbered heading below, with the code cell under it. Its first line says its kind: it changes only files, or only prints; it is a call to the warehouse, yours to run; or it is a decision, yours to make.
  • Upgrade: the Project action that carries a Project's files from the Toolbox version that last wrote them to the one copied in, one version at a time.
  • UPGRADE mark: a # UPGRADE comment the Upgrade puts at a line it leaves for you to change.
  • Prod Database: the database others read your finished Saved tables from, which a table reaches only once promoted from the Dev Database: PROD in admin/databases.py.
  • Saved table: a table in the warehouse that only your own code writes, its files in a folder of its own.
  • Project action: a one-time change to a Project's files, run from the admin folder or a Workflow's notebook, and recorded in admin/admin_log.md.
  • Dev Database: the database where you create and write tables of your own, each built, written and compared there first: DEV in admin/databases.py.

Decision rules

  • If you run the steps yourself, run each step's cell in turn, in order.
  • If you want them run for you, run the last cell, project.run_workflow(...), in place of their cells. It runs every step left in turn, up to the first only you can do, and says where it stopped: go on from that step.

Self-check

Once the last step is done, each of these holds:

  • admin/project_version.txt names the Toolbox version you copied in.
  • No # UPGRADE mark is left in the Project's own files.
  • project.check() lists no UPGRADE mark and no upgrade to make.

building_blocks/costs_per_job.py

Show its 56 lines
"""costs_per_job - each job's cost in cents per day billed, all regions added up.

Why: mart.job_day_costs saves these numbers, and writing them once here means a check of a
day, or another table, adds them up the same way.

ops.region_costs writes its days like 20260924, where the project's other tables write
2026-09-24. A day is compared as it is written, so the days asked for, like "2026-09-24", are
written the table's way first, like "20260924", and the day each row holds is written back the
project's way, like 2026-09-24, as dt.

The table is partitioned by region, then dt, and a read must filter one of them: this filters
dt, since reading every region is what this needs, and a job billed in two regions has a row in
each, which this adds up.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Column, Table, between, derived, hive_function,
    statement, sum_of,
)
from references.ops__region_costs import region_costs


def as_written_here(day: str) -> str:
    """A day written like ops.region_costs writes it: as_written_here("2026-09-24") is
    "20260924"."""
    return day.replace("-", "")


def dashed(day: Column) -> Column:
    """A day column written like 20260924, written like 2026-09-24 instead, in Hive."""
    return hive_function("concat", hive_function("substr", day, 1, 4), "-",
                         hive_function("substr", day, 5, 2), "-",
                         hive_function("substr", day, 7, 2))


def costs_per_job(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Table:
    """One row per job and day it was billed from first_day to last_day: its cost in cents.

    first_day and last_day are written like "2026-09-24", as everywhere else in the project,
    and so is dt, the day each row holds, so mart.job_day_costs saves its days like
    2026-09-24 too.

    sum_of leaves out NULL, so a day whose only bill hasn't come in yet is NULL, not 0: no
    cost is known yet, which isn't the same as costing nothing.
    """
    return derived(
        "costs_per_job",
        statement(
            SELECT(region_costs.job_id, AS(sum_of(region_costs.cost_cents), "cost_cents"),
                   AS(dashed(region_costs.dt), "dt")),
            FROM(region_costs),
            WHERE(between(region_costs.dt, as_written_here(first_day),
                          as_written_here(last_day))),
            GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id, "dt"),  # "dt": the day as SELECT writes it
        ),
    )

building_blocks/days_rewritten.py

Show its 20 lines
"""days_rewritten - the days each run writes again: its own, and the two before it.

Why: a bill or an event can come in a day or two after its day was saved, so each table of
the project that counts or adds up rows writes its last 3 days on each run, and naming the 3
once here keeps them alike.

A day written again replaces itself, so writing a day twice does no harm.
mart.job_events_checks writes only its day, since a check is of what was in on the day it ran.
"""

import datetime

DAYS_REWRITTEN = 3  # each run writes its own day and the 2 days before it


def first_day_written(day: str) -> str:
    """The first day a run for `day` writes: first_day_written("2026-09-24") is
    "2026-09-22"."""
    first_day = datetime.date.fromisoformat(day) - datetime.timedelta(days=DAYS_REWRITTEN - 1)
    return first_day.isoformat()

building_blocks/events_per_job.py

Show its 45 lines
"""events_per_job - each job's events, runs and minutes for each dt it reads.

Why: mart.job_day_facts saves these numbers, and mart.team_days' notebook checks a day from
them too, and writing them once here means the two always count them the same way.

A run is counted at its start, and at a restart, the event the scheduler sends from 2026-09-26
on when someone starts a job again by hand: changed an hour after the Project was made, so the
Lineage difference in admin/lineage/ shows what it changed (Toolbox 8.8).

A run's minutes are the minutes since it started, on the event that ends it: finish or fail,
which ops.job_events' Alias ends_a_run names. A day with no run ending has 0 minutes, not
NULL.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, SELECT, WHERE, Table, between, count_rows, derived, fill_null,
    is_in, statement, sum_of,
)
from references.ops__job_events import ends_a_run, job_events  # ends_a_run: an Alias


def events_per_job(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Table:
    """One row per job and dt from first_day to last_day: its events, runs and minutes.

    The day, dt, ops.job_events' partition column, is kept in GROUP_BY, so each day's numbers
    stay apart when first_day and last_day are days apart, and in SELECT, so a Statement that
    reads this can join each row to another table on its day, and a write that reads it fills
    each day's Partition from it.
    """
    return derived(
        "events_per_job",
        statement(
            SELECT(
                job_events.job_id,
                job_events.dt,
                AS(count_rows(), "events"),
                AS(count_rows(where=is_in(job_events.event_type, ["start", "restart"])),
                   "runs"),
                AS(fill_null(sum_of(job_events.minutes, where=ends_a_run), 0), "minutes"),
            ),
            FROM(job_events),
            WHERE(between(job_events.dt, first_day, last_day)),
            GROUP_BY(job_events.job_id, job_events.dt),
        ),
    )

building_blocks/owner_on_day.py

Show its 32 lines
"""owner_on_day - the team that owned each job on each day, joined as of that day.

Why: mart.job_day_facts saves each job's day beside its team then, and mart.team_days'
notebook checks a day the same way, so both look the team up alike.

ops.job_owners holds a snapshot a day: each job's team and owner that day. A job can change
teams, so a row is joined to the snapshot of its own day, not the newest.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import LEFT_JOIN, Clause, Column, all_of, between, equals
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners


def owner_on_day(job_id_column: Column, day_column: Column, first_day: str,
                 last_day: str) -> Clause:
    """LEFT_JOIN ops.job_owners on the job and the day, so job_owners.team is the team then.

    job_id_column and day_column are the reading Statement's columns, such as events.job_id
    and events.dt; first_day and last_day are the days it reads, written like "2026-09-24".
    Every read of a partitioned table must filter one of its partition columns, a joined
    table's too, so they filter job_owners' dt here, in ON=, and the match on the day keeps
    each row to its own day's snapshot.

    It is a LEFT_JOIN, so a row whose day has no snapshot yet keeps its numbers, with a NULL
    team, rather than being dropped. ON= names job_owners' whole key, the job and the day, so
    each row matches one snapshot row at most, and nothing is counted twice.
    """
    return LEFT_JOIN(
        job_owners,
        ON=all_of(equals(job_owners.job_id, job_id_column), equals(job_owners.dt, day_column),
                  between(job_owners.dt, first_day, last_day)),
    )

references/mart.py

Show its 47 lines
"""mart - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import mart` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as mart.budget_overruns.job_id.

Its tables: budget_overruns (references/mart__budget_overruns.py), job_day_costs
(references/mart__job_day_costs.py), job_day_facts (references/mart__job_day_facts.py),
job_events_checks (references/mart__job_events_checks.py), job_hours
(references/mart__job_hours.py), job_notes (references/mart__job_notes.py), team_days
(references/mart__team_days.py), team_failures (references/mart__team_failures.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs
from references.mart__job_day_facts import job_day_facts
from references.mart__job_events_checks import job_events_checks
from references.mart__job_hours import job_hours
from references.mart__job_notes import job_notes
from references.mart__team_days import team_days
from references.mart__team_failures import team_failures

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.mart import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["budget_overruns", "job_day_costs", "job_day_facts", "job_events_checks", "job_hours", "job_notes", "team_days", "team_failures"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"mart has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

references/mart__budget_overruns.py

Show its 47 lines
"""mart.budget_overruns - one row per job and region: its budget and days over it.

Why: its notebook saves, for each region, how often each job's daily cost went over its
budget there, so whoever pays for a region reads its own Partition without adding up every
bill again.

It is partitioned by region, the region the job is billed in, as ops.region_budgets is, and
holds no day: each write replaces a region's Partition whole, with the days its notebook's
FIRST_DAY and LAST_DAY name.

project.new_table wrote this file from admin/templates/saved_table_reference.py; it was
filled in by hand, since the table doesn't exist until its notebook's create() makes it.
"""

from sqlglot_composer import Table

budget_overruns = Table(
    "mart.budget_overruns",
    columns={
        "job_id": "bigint",
        "budget_cents": "bigint",
        "days": "bigint",
        "days_over": "bigint",
        "region": "string",
    },
    partitioned_by=["region"],
    key=["job_id", "region"],
    location="/data/your_team/mart.db/budget_overruns",  # its own folder
    descriptions={
        "budget_cents": "the most a day of the job should cost in the region",
        "days": "the days the job has a cost row in the region, its bill in or not",
        "days_over": "those whose cost was more than the budget",
        "region": "where the job is billed: eu or us",
    },
)

# --- Aliases ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Your own names for this table's columns and conditions, written by hand below.
# Import one beside the table, such as:
#     from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns, <alias>
#
# A column Alias is a shorter name for one column, for you: the Hive, and the DataFrame you
# get back, still call the column by its own name. To rename an output, use AS(...).
#     <alias> = budget_overruns.<column>
# A condition Alias names a condition on this table alone, for WHERE or
# count_rows(where=...). Add what it calls, such as equals, to the import at the top.

references/mart__job_hours.py

Show its 63 lines
"""mart.job_hours - one row per job and team: the hours its runs took.

Why: the project's notebooks read the table through this file, so each column they name, and the
key each join and count relies on, where it has one, is checked as the Statement is built.

project.convert wrote this file from the workbook's PivotTable, its columns from the Hive the
workbook gave; the convert Workflow's decisions cell then answered its name, its location and
its Pivot filter, and accepted its proposed Key. Its two TODO lines were then filled in by hand:
this docstring's first line and does_not_add_up.
"""

    "mart.job_hours",

        "job_id": "bigint",

    does_not_add_up=[],
)

# decisions cell: "proposed" keeps it; a name of your own renames this table, and its
# Total Row's table, if it has one, with it.
NAME = "job_hours"  # for PivotTable1 of job_hours.xlsx

# The database table each Table or range of the workbook it reads samples, proposed from
# its name, which needn't be the one your warehouse gives it. Answer "sources" in the
# decisions cell with the table it samples, in any database, such as {"ops.table1":
# "ops.job_runs"}, or {"ops.table1": "proposed"} to keep it.
SOURCES = {
    "ops.job_events": "ops.job_events",  # the database table job_events of job_hours.xlsx samples
    "ops.job_owners": "ops.job_owners",  # the database table job_owners of job_hours.xlsx samples
}

# What the PivotTable this table was made from leaves out: each Pivot filter, by its
# field, a Decision. Answer "pivot_filters" in the decisions cell: "keep" keeps the
# selection as converted; "fold", "batch" or "tables" keeps every value instead, as its
# checklist says.
PIVOT_FILTERS = {
    "team": "fold",  # the slicer on team keeps data. "keep": 1 table, that selection; "fold": 1 table, every team; "batch": 1 table, a Partition per team; "tables": at least 2 tables, one per team the PivotTable's cache held (data and finance)
}

# Rebuilt or History: "rebuilt" if each write replaces the whole table; "history" if
# each write keeps the rows already there, adding rows or replacing some, such as one
# day's Partition, so the table needs its past rows loaded first, its Seed. Answer
# "kind" in the decisions cell: "proposed" accepts the kind proposed below.
KIND = "rebuilt"  # TODO: proposed rebuilt: it is a query alone, which each write writes whole

references/ops.py

Show its 42 lines
"""ops - a Database: written by the Project actions, never by hand.

Why: `from references import ops` gives each of its Table references by its name, so a column
is reached as ops.job_events.event_id.

Its tables: job_events (references/ops__job_events.py), job_owners
(references/ops__job_owners.py), region_budgets (references/ops__region_budgets.py),
region_costs (references/ops__region_costs.py), team_notes (references/ops__team_notes.py).

The Project actions and refresh_reference_sheet write this file again from the Table
references in references/, so a change made here is lost. A Table reference with a <...> still
in it, as new_table writes one, is left out until it is filled in.
"""

import difflib
from typing import NoReturn

from references.ops__job_events import job_events
from references.ops__job_owners import job_owners
from references.ops__region_budgets import region_budgets
from references.ops__region_costs import region_costs
from references.ops__team_notes import team_notes

# Its tables, by the names tab completion lists and a wrong name's message gives: the names
# `from references.ops import *` gives, too.
__all__ = ["job_events", "job_owners", "region_budgets", "region_costs", "team_notes"]
_TABLES = __all__


def __getattr__(name: str) -> NoReturn:
    """A name that isn't one of its tables: say which tables it has, and the closest."""
    if name.startswith("_"):
        raise AttributeError(name)
    close = difflib.get_close_matches(name, _TABLES, n=1)
    hint = f" Did you mean {close[0]!r}?" if close else ""
    raise AttributeError(f"ops has no table {name!r}.{hint} Its tables are: "
                         f"{', '.join(_TABLES)}.", obj=None)


def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    """Its tables, so tab completion lists them."""
    return list(_TABLES)

scheduled_runs/weekly_team/weekly_team.hql

Show its 115 lines
-- The Hive of the Scheduled run weekly_team, generated by
-- project.export_run_hive("weekly_team") from weekly_team.ipynb, beside this file, built
-- as its Build cell builds it, with the defaults of its # parameters cell, sending
-- nothing. It is to read, never to edit or to send: the notebook builds its Hive again
-- each time it runs, its Run values worked out then, so this file is never what is sent.
-- To change it, edit the notebook, save it and call
-- project.export_run_hive("weekly_team") again. It holds 4 Statements.
--
-- Parameters, as this run uses them:
--   START_FROM = ""
--   WEEK = None

-- 1 of 4: mart.job_day_costs (read by mart.team_days)
-- Session settings: dynamic_partitions, sent one line at a time
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
WITH costs_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    region_costs.job_id,
    SUM(region_costs.cost_cents) AS cost_cents,
    CONCAT(
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 1, 4),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 5, 2),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 7, 2)
    ) AS dt
  FROM ops.region_costs AS region_costs
  WHERE
    region_costs.dt BETWEEN '20260922' AND '20260924'
  GROUP BY
    region_costs.job_id,
    CONCAT(
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 1, 4),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 5, 2),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 7, 2)
    )
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_costs PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  costs_per_job.job_id,
  costs_per_job.cost_cents,
  costs_per_job.dt
FROM costs_per_job;

-- 2 of 4: mart.job_day_facts (read by mart.team_days)
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type = 'start' THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(
      SUM(
        CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END
      ),
      0
    ) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  events_per_job.job_id,
  job_owners.team,
  events_per_job.events,
  events_per_job.runs,
  events_per_job.minutes,
  events_per_job.dt
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24';

-- 3 of 4: mart.team_days (read by week_totals)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.team_days PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  job_day_facts.team,
  SUM(job_day_facts.events) AS events,
  SUM(job_day_facts.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(job_day_facts.minutes) AS minutes,
  SUM(job_day_costs.cost_cents) AS cost_cents,
  job_day_facts.dt
FROM mart.job_day_facts AS job_day_facts
LEFT JOIN mart.job_day_costs AS job_day_costs
  ON job_day_costs.job_id = job_day_facts.job_id
  AND job_day_costs.dt = job_day_facts.dt
  AND job_day_costs.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
WHERE
  job_day_facts.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
GROUP BY
  job_day_facts.dt,
  job_day_facts.team;

-- 4 of 4: week_totals
SELECT
  CAST(NEXT_DAY(DATE_ADD(team_days.dt, 7 * -1), 'MO') AS STRING) AS week,
  team_days.team,
  SUM(team_days.events) AS events,
  SUM(team_days.runs) AS runs,
  SUM(team_days.minutes) AS minutes,
  SUM(team_days.cost_cents) AS cost_cents
FROM mart.team_days AS team_days
WHERE
  team_days.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-21' AND '2026-09-27'
GROUP BY
  CAST(NEXT_DAY(DATE_ADD(team_days.dt, 7 * -1), 'MO') AS STRING),
  team_days.team;

scheduled_runs/weekly_team/weekly_team.ipynb

Scheduled run: weekly_team

Fill in every <...> in the code cells below, brackets and all: the notebook won't run with one left. They are: - <RESULT_NAME>: the name a SELECT's rows come back by, in quotes, such as "week_totals"; - <SAVED_TABLE>: a Saved table whose Table notebook's logic it uses, such as mart.team_days; - <LOGIC_FUNCTION>: the function of that logic it calls, with what it takes, such as week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY), two parameters, or write().

Follow the steps in order, as written.

Terms

  • Scheduled run: this notebook, which an outside scheduler runs top to bottom: building the Statements and their Hive in the Build cell, then sending them in the Run cell.
  • Parameter: a value the scheduler passes in, one of the names in the # parameters cell, read by its type hint: "2026-11-01" given for a datetime.date is that date.
  • Run value: what a Python function gives each time a Statement is built, such as the latest Partition of a table; run_values= gives one a value of yours for this run.

Steps

1. Name the parameters. Goal: each value the scheduler passes in. Action: write each in the # parameters cell as a name, its type hint and its default, such as RUN_DATE: datetime.date | None = None; keep START_FROM. Done when each has a hint; then step 2. 2. Connect. Goal: this run's account. Action: in the setup cell, write your connection in the connect(...) line and delete its #. Done when the setup cell runs; then step 3. 3. Name the Statements. Goal: what this run sends. Action: write each in the dict statements() gives, in the # scheduled cell, by name. If it is a Table notebook's logic, load it: project.load("mart.team_days").week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY). If it is your own, compose it there, with statement(...). Done when each is named; then step 4. 4. Build. Goal: the Hive, in order, to read before anything is sent. Action: run the cells down to the Build cell. If a table it would write is written elsewhere, name it in leave_out=. Done when it prints each Statement's Hive in order; then step 5. 5. Run. Goal: the Statements sent and the SELECTs' rows back. Action: run the Run cell, or have the scheduler run the notebook. Done when it ends with no error; each SELECT's rows are in results by the name statements() gives it, such as results["week_totals"].

What the build prints

First each parameter as used, then each Statement in the order it is sent, headed like -- 3 of 7: mart.team_days (read by week_totals), and its Hive. The writes of each table of this Project the Statements read are added, all the way up, before what reads them.

Made by project.new_scheduled_run("weekly_team") from admin/templates/scheduled_run.py.

Self-check

  • No code cell holds a <...>.
  • Each parameter in the # parameters cell has a type hint.
  • The Build cell prints each Statement's Hive, in order, and sends nothing.

Code cell 1

# parameters
# The values a scheduler passes in, each a name, its type hint and its default. A scheduler
# passes text, which the Build cell reads by the hint, and refuses a value it can't read. The
# hints it reads: datetime.date, int, float, bool, str, each alone, in list[...] or with
# | None, such as list[datetime.date] for "2026-11-01,2026-11-08".
import datetime  # for a datetime.date hint

START_FROM: str = ""  # a table to start from, as a run that failed prints it; "" sends all
WEEK: datetime.date | None = None  # a day of the week to add up, such as "2026-09-23"; None
# adds up the week of the latest day ops.job_events holds

Code cell 3

# scheduled
# The Statements this run sends, each by its name: a SELECT's rows come back in results by it.
# A Table notebook's logic gives them through project.load, such as
#     "week_totals": project.load("mart.team_days").week_totals(FIRST_DAY, LAST_DAY),
# and a Statement of your own is composed here with statement(...). A value worked out each
# time the run is built goes in as run_value(function), a function of this cell, such as
# week_totals(run_value(week_first_day), ...): import run_value from sqlglot_composer here.
from sqlglot_composer import latest_partition, run_value
from references.ops__job_events import job_events


def week_first_day() -> datetime.date:
    """The Monday that starts WEEK's week, or, when WEEK is None, the week of the latest day
    ops.job_events holds, the day the tables this run writes end on."""
    if WEEK is None:
        day = latest_partition(job_events.dt, query)
    else:
        day = WEEK
    return day - datetime.timedelta(days=day.weekday())


def week_last_day() -> datetime.date:
    """The Sunday that ends the week week_first_day starts."""
    return week_first_day() + datetime.timedelta(days=6)


def statements() -> dict[str, Statement]:
    """What this run sends, by name: the build adds the writes of each table they read."""
    team_days = project.load("mart.team_days")
    return {
        "week_totals": team_days.week_totals(run_value(week_first_day),
                                             run_value(week_last_day)),
    }

# leave_out=["mart.daily_job_runs"] leaves out that table's write, and what only it reads.

tables/mart__budget_overruns/mart__budget_overruns.ipynb

mart.budget_overruns

Each job's budget in each region it is billed in, and how many of its days cost more, saved a region at a time.

Why: a region's budget holder reads only that region's rows, so the table is partitioned by region, and each region's Partition is written on its own.

Made by project.new_table("mart.budget_overruns") from admin/templates/saved_table.py, then filled in. Its write() gives one write per region, each naming its Partition with partition={"region": ...}: the region is then a value of the write, not a column of its SELECT, and each write replaces that region's Partition alone. ops.region_costs writes its days like 20260924, so FIRST_DAY and LAST_DAY are written its way by as_written_here, from building_blocks/costs_per_job.py. A bill not in yet, NULL, is never over budget.

The code cells: 1 the Project and your query, connected by connect(...); 2 the logic, which starts # logic; 3 try it; 4 its lineage; 5 its Hive, in mart__budget_overruns.hql; 6 its rows in the Reference sheet.

Code cell 1

# 1. The Project and your query
import pathlib
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, str(pathlib.Path.cwd().parents[1]))  # the Project folder: two up from here

from sqlglot_composer import export_hive, export_lineage, project, run_query, show_hive
from warehouse import connect, query

# Your connection to the warehouse. Each notebook runs on its own, so each connects here, at
# its top, before it calls query or execute: write yours in the line below and delete its #,
# such as connect(your_library.connect(...)). A connection kept in a file outside the Project,
# such as team_connection.py, is imported by these lines, written in that line's place:
#     sys.path.append("C:/work/connections")  # the folder team_connection.py is in
#     from team_connection import conn
#     connect(conn)
connect(conn)  # conn: your team's connection; here, the Example database's

Code cell 2

# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the regions and days it writes, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    AS, FROM, GROUP_BY, INSERT_OVERWRITE, JOIN, SELECT, WHERE, Clause, Statement, all_of,
    between, count_rows, create_table, equals, more_than, statement,
)
from building_blocks.costs_per_job import as_written_here
from references.mart__budget_overruns import budget_overruns
from references.ops__region_budgets import region_budgets
from references.ops__region_costs import region_costs

REGIONS = ["eu", "us"]  # the Partitions write() writes, one write each
FIRST_DAY = "2026-09-11"  # the days of ops.region_costs it counts: the Example database's 14
LAST_DAY = "2026-09-24"


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.budget_overruns from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(budget_overruns, may_exist=True)


def rows(region: str) -> list[Clause]:
    """One region's rows, the clauses preview and write share: each job billed there, its
    budget, its days and the days it went over. Both tables are partitioned by region, so each
    filters it, region_budgets in JOIN's ON=; region_costs filters its dt too."""
    return [
        SELECT(
            region_costs.job_id,
            region_budgets.budget_cents,
            AS(count_rows(), "days"),
            AS(count_rows(where=more_than(region_costs.cost_cents,
                                          region_budgets.budget_cents)), "days_over"),
        ),
        FROM(region_costs),
        JOIN(region_budgets, ON=all_of(equals(region_budgets.job_id, region_costs.job_id),
                                       equals(region_budgets.region, region))),
        WHERE(equals(region_costs.region, region),
              between(region_costs.dt, as_written_here(FIRST_DAY), as_written_here(LAST_DAY))),
        GROUP_BY(region_costs.job_id, region_budgets.budget_cents),
    ]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save in the first region, as a SELECT to run and check
    first."""
    return statement(*rows(REGIONS[0]))


def write() -> list[Statement]:
    """Step 3: a write per region, each replacing that region's Partition alone."""
    return [statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(budget_overruns, partition={"region": region}),
                      *rows(region))
            for region in REGIONS]

It shows nothing.

-- The Hive of mart.job_day_costs, generated by export_hive() from the logic cell of
-- mart__job_day_costs.ipynb, beside this file. Edit the logic there, not this file: each
-- export_hive() writes it again, from the notebook as last saved. It holds 2 steps, in
-- the order they are sent: create() and write().

  job_id BIGINT,
  cost_cents BIGINT,

  dt STRING
)

SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
WITH costs_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    region_costs.job_id,
    SUM(region_costs.cost_cents) AS cost_cents,
    CONCAT(
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 1, 4),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 5, 2),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 7, 2)
    ) AS dt
  FROM ops.region_costs AS region_costs
  WHERE
    region_costs.dt BETWEEN '20260922' AND '20260924'
  GROUP BY
    region_costs.job_id,
    CONCAT(
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 1, 4),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 5, 2),
      '-',
      SUBSTRING(region_costs.dt, 7, 2)
    )
)

  costs_per_job.job_id,
  costs_per_job.cost_cents,
  costs_per_job.dt
FROM costs_per_job;

tables/mart__job_day_costs/mart__job_day_costs.ipynb

mart.job_day_costs

Each job's cost per day, the last 3 days saved again on every run.

Why: a day's bill can come in after the day was saved, so each run saves the last few days again, and a day saved twice must hold the same rows, not twice as many.

Made by project.new_table("mart.job_day_costs") from admin/templates/saved_table.py, then filled in, its rows() taking the days it writes, since it writes several: an incremental load, each run writing its last 3 days again. Its rows come from the Building block building_blocks/costs_per_job.py, and the days each run writes from building_blocks/days_rewritten.py, which each counting table of the project shares. A bill lands in the partition of the day it is for, but can come in later: invoice_sync's (job 2's) for 2026-09-24 is still NULL in ops.region_costs, and the next run that writes the day again saves it.

ops.region_costs writes its days like 20260924; this table writes them like 2026-09-24, as the project's other tables do, so mart.team_days can join it to them on the day: the Building block writes each day the project's way.

The code cells: 1 the Project and your query, connected by connect(...); 2 the logic, which starts # logic; 3 try it; 4 its lineage; 5 its Hive, in mart__job_day_costs.hql; 6 its rows in the Reference sheet.

# logic
# 2. The logic: imports, the days it writes, and functions only
from sqlglot_composer import (
    FROM, INSERT_OVERWRITE, SELECT, Clause, Statement, create_table, statement,
)
from building_blocks.costs_per_job import costs_per_job
from building_blocks.days_rewritten import first_day_written
from references.mart__job_day_costs import job_day_costs

DAY = "2026-09-24"  # the last day write() writes, the Example database's last day


def create() -> Statement:
    """Step 1: create mart.job_day_costs from its Table reference, if it isn't there yet."""
    return create_table(job_day_costs, may_exist=True)


def rows(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> list[Clause]:
    """The clauses every step below shares: each job's cost per day. The day comes last in
    SELECT: INSERT_OVERWRITE writes each day's rows into that day's Partition, and replaces
    only the days the rows hold."""
    costs = costs_per_job(first_day, last_day)
    return [SELECT(costs.job_id, costs.cost_cents, costs.dt), FROM(costs)]


def preview() -> Statement:
    """Step 2: the rows write() would save, as a SELECT to run and check first."""
    return statement(*rows(first_day_written(DAY), DAY))


def write_days(first_day: str, last_day: str) -> Statement:
    """Every day from first_day to last_day, in one write: also a backfill, given an earlier
    first_day."""
    return statement(INSERT_OVERWRITE(job_day_costs), *rows(first_day, last_day))


def write() -> Statement:
    """Step 3, every run: the last 3 days, ending on DAY, in one write."""
    return write_days(first_day_written(DAY), DAY)

Show its 56 lines
-- The Hive of mart.job_day_facts, generated by export_hive() from the logic cell of
-- mart__job_day_facts.ipynb, beside this file. Edit the logic there, not this file: each
-- export_hive() writes it again, from the notebook as last saved. It holds 2 steps, in
-- the order they are sent: create() and write().

-- 1 of 2: create()
CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE IF NOT EXISTS mart.job_day_facts (
  job_id BIGINT,
  team STRING,
  events BIGINT,
  runs BIGINT,
  minutes BIGINT,
  PRIMARY KEY (job_id, dt) DISABLE NOVALIDATE
)
PARTITIONED BY (
  dt STRING
)
STORED AS ORC
LOCATION '/data/your_team/mart.db/job_day_facts';

-- 2 of 2: write()
-- The Session setting "dynamic_partitions": this write fills dt from its rows.
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true;
SET hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict;
WITH events_per_job AS (
  SELECT
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt,
    COUNT(*) AS events,
    COUNT(CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('start', 'restart') THEN 1 END) AS runs,
    COALESCE(
      SUM(
        CASE WHEN job_events.event_type IN ('finish', 'fail') THEN job_events.minutes END
      ),
      0
    ) AS minutes
  FROM ops.job_events AS job_events
  WHERE
    job_events.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24'
  GROUP BY
    job_events.job_id,
    job_events.dt
)
INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE mart.job_day_facts PARTITION(dt)
SELECT
  events_per_job.job_id,
  job_owners.team,
  events_per_job.events,
  events_per_job.runs,
  events_per_job.minutes,
  events_per_job.dt
FROM events_per_job
LEFT JOIN ops.job_owners AS job_owners
  ON job_owners.job_id = events_per_job.job_id
  AND job_owners.dt = events_per_job.dt
  AND job_owners.dt BETWEEN '2026-09-22' AND '2026-09-24';

tables/mart__job_day_facts/mart__job_day_facts.ipynb

mart.job_day_facts

Each job's events, runs and minutes per day, beside the team that owned the job that day, the last 3 days saved again on every run.

Why: a job can change teams, so counting a team's day means looking each job up as of that day, which is worth doing once and saving.

Made by project.new_table("mart.job_day_facts") from admin/templates/saved_table.py, then filled in, writing its last 3 days on each run, as mart.job_day_costs does: an as-of lookup, each row joined to the snapshot of its own day. Its rows come from the Building block building_blocks/events_per_job.py, joined to the day's team by building_blocks/owner_on_day.py. A day with no snapshot in ops.job_owners keeps its numbers with a NULL team.

The code cells: 1 the Project and your query, connected by connect(...); 2 the logic, which starts # logic; 3 try it; 4 its lineage; 5 its Hive, in mart__job_day_facts.hql; 6 its rows in the Reference sheet.
